State of the Market 2024

Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

1  EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 

Executive summary 

Many of the themes that have been identified in previous years – resource adequacy challenges 

and increasing renewable generation – persist in 2024. In addition, the market continues to see 

escalating load growth with the high likelihood of this continuing in future years. One factor for 

this is the growing energy needs and changing load patterns related to data centers supporting 

AI technology and crypto mines. Variable energy resources continue to play an increasing role in 

the Southwest Power Pool (SPP) markets. This has contributed to outcomes such as increasing 

variability and uncertainty of supply, out-of-market actions to ensure system reliability, higher 

make-whole payments, and negative prices. Natural gas prices have reduced after reaching high 

levels in 2022. The MMU continues to find the SPP market to be competitive in 2024. 

Some key observations in the SPP Integrated Marketplace during 2024: 

•  Gas prices decreased. The average gas price for 2024 at the Panhandle Eastern hub was 

$1.81/MMBtu, a decrease of 16% from 2023 when the average price was $2.16/MMBtu. 

•  Day-ahead market and real-time market prices were down. The average hourly day-

ahead price decreased by 3% from $28.45/MWh in 2023 to $27.56/MWh in 2024 and the 

real-time price decreased by 4% from $27.56/MWh in 2023 to $26.18/MWh in 2024. 

•  Addition of wind resources has slowed. In 2024, 1,083 MW nameplate capacity of new 

wind resources was added to the market. In comparison, in 2021, 3,200 MW of 

nameplate capacity was added. However, the generation interconnection queue still 

contains about 30 GW of new wind resources to potentially be added to the market. 

•  Solar, battery, and hybrid resources are a large portion of the generation 

interconnection queue. At the end of 2024, solar resources totaling 44 GW of nameplate 

capacity, battery resources totaling 25 GW of nameplate capacity, and hybrid resources 

(typically solar plus battery) totaling 14 GW of nameplate capacity are in the generation 

interconnection queue. This represents 65% of the entire generation interconnection 

queue. 

•  Resource adequacy issues persist. The winter weather events that occurred in February 

2021, December 2022, and January 2024 highlighted that while the availability and 

performance of our accredited resources are likely improving, SPP still faces significant 

capacity risk during these events. The MMU outlined several of these issues in our winter 

weather reports. Outside of winter, resource advisories and conservative operations have 

been declared during periods of mild weather when a combination of outages and a 

lower wind output have led to tight conditions. The current supply adequacy approach 

State of the Market 2024 

1 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Executive summary 

does not sufficiently address supply-related risks, nor does it sufficiently assign the costs 

of supply adequacy in accordance with cost causation principles. Deficiencies in the 

current design include a lack of a seasonal resource adequacy requirement, an 

incomplete accounting of correlated output and outages among similar resources, and 

an accreditation process that does not reflect actual resource performance. 

•  Revenue neutrality uplift decreased. Revenue neutrality uplift dropped from 2023 levels. 

Revenue neutrality uplift for 2024 was $163 million, down 21% from $205 million in 2023 

and down from $563 million in 2022. Most of the decrease can be attributed to a 

decrease in the real-time congestion component of RNU and an increase in the joint 

operating agreement component of RNU. 

•  Make-whole payments increased in the day-ahead but decreased in reliability unit 

commitment. Day-ahead make-whole payments increased from $79 million in 2023 to 

$119 million in 2024, an increase of 51%. Most of the increase can be attributed to the 

winter weather event in January 2024. Reliability unit commitment make-whole 

payments were down 12%, from $157 million in 2023 to $138 million in 2024. Much of 

this decrease can be attributed to the decrease in natural gas prices.  

•  Wind penetration increased. Installed nameplate wind capacity stood at 34,808 MW at 

the end of 2024. Wind generation capacity now accounts for 34% of installed nameplate 

capacity in the SPP market. 

•  Generation mix continued to evolve. For the third straight year, and four years out of 

the past five, wind generation produced the highest percentage of total generation, at 

38% in 2024. Coal generation dropped from 33% of total generation in 2022 to 27% in 

2023 and to 25% in 2024. 

•  Negative price intervals increased. The frequency of negative price intervals increased in 

the day-ahead market from 6.3% of asset owner intervals in 2023 to 9.9% in 2024. In the 

real-time market negative price intervals increased from 11.8% in 2023 to 15.2% in 2024. 

Most of the increase can be attributed to increased wind generation year-over-year. 

•  Congestion payments increased. Total congestion payments for 2024 were over $1.8 

billion, up from $1.4 billion in 2023 but still down from $2 billion in 2022. 

•  Transmission congestion rights funding fell outside of the targeted range. 

Transmission congestion rights (TCR) funding in 2024 fell outside the target range of 

90%, coming in at 83%, down from 88% in 2022 and 2023.  

State of the Market 2024 

2 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Executive summary 

•  Demand response capacity increased. At the end of 2024, there was 968 MW of 

dispatchable demand response resources of installed nameplate capacity representing 

121 resources. This is an increase of 170 MW nameplate capacity, but a net loss of 12 

resources. 

•  Market-to-market payments from MISO were up. Net market-to-market payments 

from MISO to SPP for 2024 were $112 million, up 20% from $93 million in 2023. 

•  Virtual profits increased. Average profit per cleared virtual megawatt after fees was 

$1.25/MW in 2024, up 13% from $1.10/MW in 2023. 

•  Outaged capacity increased. Total outages and derates for capacity taken out-of-service 

increased by 8% from 2023 to 2024, with forced outages and derates increasing by 14% 

and maintenance outages and derates increasing by 1%. 

•  Energy consumption increased. Monthly average system energy consumption was up 

3% compared to 2023. Most of this increase can be attributed to load growth during the 

year, as opposed to weather impacts. 

•  Self-committed capacity remained flat. The average percentage of day-ahead market 

capacity by commitment status shows that self-commitment stayed flat at 15% of 

capacity, while market status dropped from 62% of committed capacity to in 2023 to 

55% in 2024. Outaged capacity, however, increased from 15% in 2023 to 21% in 2024. 

•  Regional congestion patterns remained the same. The areas that experienced the 

highest congestion costs in 2024 were the northwest corner of North Dakota, eastern 

Oklahoma, a concentrated area in southeast North Dakota near the MISO seam, and 

eastern New Mexico. Much of Kansas, Nebraska, South Dakota, and northwest Oklahoma 

experienced the lowest congestion costs for the year. These patterns are very similar to 

those experienced in 2023. 

•  Overall, hedging covered congestion costs. In aggregate, load-serving entities were 

over-hedged at 158% of their congestion cost and non-load-serving entities hedged at 

55% of their total congestion cost. 

• 

Individual congesting hedging ranged in performance. Individual market participants 

hedged congestion with varying degrees of effectiveness. Overall, 79% of load-serving 

entities recovered all of their congestion costs through the auction revenue right and 

transmission congestion right auctions. 

State of the Market 2024 

3 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Executive summary 

•  Auction revenue right funding increased. Auction revenue right funding increased to 

125% in 2024, up from 118% in 2023. The ARR surplus increased by just over $45 million 

year-over-year. 

•  SPP markets remain competitive. Structural and behavioral metrics indicate that the SPP 

markets have been competitive over the last several years. The market share, Herfindahl-

Hirschman Index (HHI), and pivotal supplier analyses all indicate minimal to moderate 

potential structural market power in SPP markets outside of frequently constrained areas. 

The HHI showed that only 2% of hours were considered moderately concentrated, this 

has been on a steady decline since 2018. 

•  Offer mitigation frequency in the day-ahead market rose for incremental energy and 

load but dropped for operating reserves. Overall, levels remain very low at less than 

0.75% across energy, no-load, and operating reserve intervals. For operating reserves, 

mitigation frequency depicts a relatively high level of concentration only in the month of 

June, largely driven by a relatively high level of mitigation frequency for spin offers. All 

operating reserve categories (reg-up, reg-down, spin, and supplemental) display a slight 

decrease in mitigation frequency relative to 2023. In 2024, the application of mitigation 

in the day-ahead market occurred at the following frequencies: no-load (0.67%), 

operating reserves (0.23%), and energy (0.04%). The low level of mitigated resource 

hours relative to market activity remains a hallmark of the day-ahead market. For the 

real-time market, the annual mitigation frequency in 2023 and 2024 decreased sharply 

compared to 2022, occurring at 0.02% in both 2023 and 2024.  

•  The exercise of market power remains rare. Behavioral measures suggest that attempts 

to exercise market power by manipulating the price (economic withholding) or quantity 

(physical withholding) of generation are rare. The output gap, an inference of 

economically withheld generation, was slightly higher in 2024. Overall, it remains at very 

low levels, averaging less than 0.2% in all months.

Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

1.1  OVERVIEW 

Executive summary 

During 2024, there were three themes that dominated the SPP markets: continued low gas 

prices, resource adequacy concerns, and increasing load growth. The average gas cost at the 

Panhandle hub decreased by 16% from 2023 to 2024, while the day-ahead average price 

decreased by 3% and the real-time average price decreased by 4%. A winter weather event in 

January and an Energy Emergency Alert (EEA) 1 in August also impacted prices in 2024. 

Typically, gas costs and electricity prices are highly correlated over time, however, the 

correlation is not 1-to-1 and we have observed this correlation weakening. In most years, the 

gas cost and the electricity price move in the same direction, however, the magnitude of change 

can differ. This can be due to changing congestion patterns, increasing load, and other market 

impacts, as was the case from 2023 to 2024. For example, the increase in negative-priced 

intervals and the reduced frequency of natural gas resources being marginal contributes to this 

lower magnitude of change.  

As with previous years, energy costs remain to be the largest component of total wholesale 

costs, which represented 96% of total costs in 2024. Historically, the percentage has remained 
around 96% since the start of the Integrated Marketplace in 2014.1  

The annual five-minute peak demand of 56,139 MW was 2.5% lower in 2024 compared to last 

year, however, total electricity consumption was up by 3%. Of the 2,352 MW net increase in 

nameplate generation capacity from 2024, 1,083 MW were from wind resources, 805 MW from 

natural gas, 402 MW from solar resources, 284 MW were from dispatchable demand response, 
130 MW from market storage resources, and 55 MW were from fuel oil resources.2  

After a brief dip from 2022 to 2023, wind generation, as a percentage of total generation, 

continued its steady climb, increasing from 37% of total generation in 2023 to 38% in 2024. 

After a rise from 2020 to 2021, coal generation continued to decrease, representing 25% of total 

generation in 2024; down from 27% in 2023. The increase in coal generation from 2020 to 2021 

was primarily the result of higher gas prices in 2021, making coal generation more economic 

than some gas resources. 

1 The 2021 winter weather event was an outlier; make-whole payments reached over $1 billion, and the 
percentage decreased to 85%. 
2 The sum of the nameplate capacity of added resources in 2024 is higher than the net amount of 
nameplate capacity added due to retirements and derates of other resources in the market.

Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Executive summary 

1.2  DAY-AHEAD AND REAL-TIME MARKET PERFORMANCE 

Overall, both day-ahead and real-time energy prices were down in 2024 compared to 2023. The 

average day-ahead price for 2024 was $27.56/MWh, a decrease of 3% from $28.45/MWh in 

2023. The average real-time price was $26.18/MWh for 2024, a decrease of 4% from 

$27.39/MWh in 2023. Much of this decrease can be attributed to the lower gas costs during 

2024. As mentioned in the key observations, the average gas price for 2024 at the Panhandle 

Eastern hub was $1.81/MMBtu, a decrease of 16% from 2023 when the average price was 

$2.16/MMBtu. 

Load participation in the day-ahead market dropped slightly in 2024. The average monthly level 

of participation for the load assets was between 96% and 99% of the actual real-time load, with 

the annual average of 97.4%., down slightly from 97.6% in 2023. Prior to 2022, the monthly level 

averaged between 98% and 101%, with an annual average of 99% in 2021. 

On average for 2024, wind generation was 3,150 MW higher in the real-time market compared 

to the amount scheduled in the day-ahead market on an hourly basis. This disconnect 

represents a persistent and escalating challenge to the market as wind generation continues its 

expansion. 

Virtual bids and offers may theoretically offset the under-scheduling of renewable supply in the 

day-ahead market. However, in net they did not, as they averaged 2,800 MW of net virtual 

supply. Furthermore, it is important to recognize that even if virtual transactions were to match 

the quantity of under-scheduled renewables, the prices associated with the virtual offers are not 

likely to fully represent the expected offer prices of renewable resources. 

In general, virtual transactions were profitable in the SPP market. Gross profit before fees was 

$209 million in 2024, up from $186 million in 2023. When charges and transaction fees are 

included, the net profit for virtual transactions was $99 million in 2024, up from $75 million in 

2023. 

Generation offered in the day-ahead market averaged just over 55% in market commitment 

status followed by self-commitment status at 15% of the total capacity commitments for 2024. 

While self-commitments were unchanged from 2023, market commitments dropped sharply 

from 62% in 2023. Capacity on outage, however, climbed from 15% in 2023 to 21% in 2024. 

1.3  TRANSMISSION CONGESTION AND HEDGING 

Locational marginal prices reflect the sum of the marginal cost of energy, the marginal cost of 

congestion, and the marginal cost of losses for each pricing interval at any given pricing location 

State of the Market 2024 

6 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Executive summary 

in the market. Certain locations of the footprint experience significant price movements 

resulting from congestion caused by high wind generation, external flows, and transmission 

limitations. 

The areas that experienced the highest congestion costs in 2024 were around Oklahoma City 

and south-central Oklahoma in the southeast part of the footprint, southeast New Mexico in the 

southwest part of the footprint, western Nebraska towards the middle of the footprint, and 

northwest North Dakota and southeast North Dakota towards the north of the footprint. Much 

of Kansas, Nebraska, and northwest Oklahoma experienced the lowest congestion costs for the 

year. 

The 2024 frequently constrained area study identified the Tulsa, Oklahoma; Oklahoma City, 

Oklahoma; and Williston, North Dakota areas as frequently constrained areas and these areas 

continued to see elevated congestion prices during 2024. 

Total congestion payments for 2024 were over $1.8 billion, up from $1.4 billion in 2023 but still 

down from $2 billion in 2022. 

While most load-serving entities were able to successfully hedge their congestion exposure with 

auction revenue rights (ARRs) and transmission congestion rights (TCRs), a handful of 

participants were under-hedged. The largest individual amount over-hedged was $89 million, 

while the largest amount under-hedged was $67 million. 

1.4  UPLIFT COSTS 

Generators receive make-whole payments to ensure that they receive sufficient revenue to cover 

energy, start-up, no-load, and operating reserve costs for both market and local reliability 

commitments. A make-whole payment is an additional market payment that supplements a 

resource’s revenue if prices fall below its cleared offer. These payments are intended to make 

resources whole to energy, commitment, and operating reserve costs. 

For 2024, combined day-ahead and reliability unit commitment (RUC) make-whole payments 

totaled $257 million. RUC make-whole payments represented 54% of the total, with day-ahead 

make-whole payments accounting for the other 46%. 

Day-ahead make-whole payments were over $119 million in 2024, up from $79 million in 2023, 

an increase of 51%. Reliability unit commitment make-whole payments in 2024 totaled $138 

million, down from $157 million in 2023, representing a 12% decrease. 

Revenue neutrality uplift (RNU) ensures settlement payments and receipts for each real-time 

hourly settlement interval equals zero. Positive RNU indicates that SPP received insufficient 

State of the Market 2024 

7 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Executive summary 

revenue, initiating additional collections from market participants. Inversely, negative RNU 

indicates that SPP received excess revenue, which must then be credited back to market 

participants. 

Total monthly revenue neutrality uplift for 2024 was $163 million, down 21% from $205 million 

in 2023. The main components of revenue neutrality uplift are real-time congestion not reflected 

in day-ahead, revenue inadequacy, and real-time joint operating agreement (also known as 

market-to-market). The joint operating agreement component generally acts to lessen revenue 

neutrality uplift, while the congestion and revenue inadequacy components generally act to 

increase revenue neutrality uplift. Real-time joint operating agreement portion of revenue 

neutrality uplift was just over $112 million in 2024, an increase of 20% from $94 million in 2023. 

The real-time congestion and revenue inadequacy components of revenue neutrality uplift was 

$257 million in 2024, a decrease of 9% from $283 million in 2023. 

1.5  COMPETITIVENESS ASSESSMENT 

Overall, structural and behavioral metrics indicate that the SPP markets have been competitive 

over the last several years. The market share, Herfindahl–Hirschman Index (HHI), and pivotal 

supplier analysis indicate minimal to moderate potential structural market power in SPP markets, 

outside of frequently congested areas.  

In 14% of the hours in 2024, the market share of the largest on-line supplier in terms of real time 

energy output exceeded 20%, a declining trend observed since 2018. Meanwhile, another 

general measure of structural market power, the Herfindahl-Hirschman Index (HHI) calculation 

for supplier concentration, shows that 2% of hours were considered moderately concentrated in 

2024 showing a decline from the 3% level in 2023 (and has similarly been on a steady decline 

since 2018). The SPP market remained mostly unconcentrated—with 90% of all intervals since 

2019 considered unconcentrated. No intervals were considered highly concentrated in 2024, and 

the percentage of intervals considered unconcentrated (98%) was at its highest level since 2017. 

Structural market power in the SPP footprint creates the potential for market manipulation. The 

MMU maintains its belief that the existing local market power mitigation measures are 

sufficiently robust to moderate the impact of an actual exercise of that potential, should it occur. 

The MMU will continue to evaluate structural market power concerns going forward. 

Any exercise of market power is most likely to be profitable in transmission-constrained areas 

where concerns regarding potential local market power are highest. MMU analysis and 

continued scrutiny of potential areas under frequent constraints confirm that existing mitigation 

measures are effective to mitigate the exercise of local market power. 

State of the Market 2024 

8 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Executive summary 

Behavioral indicators were also assessed by analyzing the conduct of market participants and 

the impact of that conduct on market prices, to detect the exercise of market power. The MMU 

examined offer price markups, offer quantities, mitigation frequency, and measures of implied 

economic and physical withholding by evaluating market behavior. The MMU noted overall 

improvements in the convergence of market price and imputed costs but still observes negative 

prices at a level and frequency that warrants continued note. In particular, wind units had 

exceptionally low markups – a concern that the MMU has raised in prior Annual State of the 

Market reports. 

Off-peak and on-peak annual average markups continued to be in negative territory in 2024, 

with the off-peak period being more pronounced. Wind resources, however, continue to offer at 

exceptionally low markup levels. 

Positive markups were observed for coal generation resources at slightly decreasing levels 

relative to the positive markups seen in 2023, but much lower than 2022. The coal markup levels 

were $4.29 in 2024, $6.88/MWh in 2023, and $21.12/MWh in 2022, respectively. While negative 

on-peak and off-peak markups were observed in almost every month for natural gas resources 

in 2024 - except for January - with a clear correlation to the prevailing price of gas within the 

SPP footprint. 

Nonetheless, all months saw negative markups for wind resources, with markup levels 

continuing to stay low throughout the year, to levels generally lower than those seen in 2023. 

The average markups for wind resources were −$37.99/MWh in 2024, −$41.50/MWh in 2023, 

and −$38.75/MWh in 2022, respectively.  

Compared with the previous year, 2024’s output gap was around 0.06 percentage points higher. 

Overall, the output gap remains at very low levels, averaging less than 0.3% in all months and 

reflects a high level of participation in the market overall. 

Incremental energy offer mitigation in 2024 stayed at roughly the same frequency in both the 

day-ahead and the real-time market, continuing to remain very rare, at 0.04% and 0.22% of 

resource hours in the day-ahead and real-time markets, respectively. However, day-ahead no-

load mitigation sharply increased because some resources with a high no-load offer relative to 

their mitigated offer hit a binding transmission constraint. The amount of physically unoffered 

generation remained at levels that are typical for this market. This is an area that could use 

improvement, however. 

Overall, the SPP Integrated Marketplace provides effective market incentives and mitigation 

measures to produce competitive market outcomes, particularly during market intervals where 

the exercise of local market power is a concern. While some behavioral indicators slightly 

State of the Market 2024 

9 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Executive summary 

increased relative to low prior-year levels, these largely reflected conditions external to the 

market, and structural indicators showed continued levels of diverse and healthy competition. 

Market behaviors and results remained workably competitive overall, only infrequently requiring 

mitigation of local market power to achieve those outcomes. Nonetheless, mitigation of 

economic withholding remains an essential tool in ensuring that market results are competitive 

during periods when such market conditions offer suppliers the potential to abuse local market 

power. 

1.6  RESOURCE ADEQUACY ISSUES 

The winter weather events that occurred in February 2021, December 2022, and January 2024 

highlighted significant issues with SPP’s resource adequacy approach. The MMU and SPP 
outlined several of these issues in our winter weather reports.3 Outside of winter, resource 

advisories and conservative operations have been declared during periods of mild weather when 

a combination of outages and lower wind output have led to tight conditions. The current 

supply adequacy approach does not sufficiently address supply-related risks, nor does it 

sufficiently assign the costs of supply adequacy according to cost causation principles, 

socializing much of the supply adequacy risk to the region. Deficiencies in the current design 

include a lack of a seasonal resource adequacy requirement, an incomplete accounting of 

correlated output and outages among similar resources, and an accreditation process that does 

not reflect actual resource performance. SPP currently has a resource adequacy requirement for 

summer and will have its first winter resource adequacy requirement in winter 2026. While this is 

a necessary first step, capacity can also be tight in the shoulder periods as resources take 

maintenance outages, something the MMU observed in the last few years. Resource adequacy is 

something that needs to be considered year-round, not just in the summer. 

Resources experienced significant fuel supply issues that limited their availability. While almost 

all fuel types experienced some issues during the winter weather events, by far some of the 

most impactful are natural gas resources in the SPP region experiencing a high level of outages 

related to fuel supply limitations. Though SPP may not experience adverse weather events every 

year, correlations in forced outages due to fuel supply shocks need to be incorporated into any 

resource adequacy approach to ensure a more accurate accounting of the expected availability 

of resources. Correlated forced outages were partially addressed in the Fuel Assurance 

3 SPP MMU Report on February 2021 Winter Weather Event, SPP's Response to the February 2021 Winter 
Weather Event, Summary, A Comprehensive Review of SPP's Response to the February 2021 Winter Storm 
report 

State of the Market 2024 

10 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Executive summary 

methodology filed with FERC in September 2024.4 However, this filing only addressed fuel-

related forced outages that are incremental to the assumed level of forced outages. Additionally, 

the filing only applies to fuel-related forced outages during the winter season. While SPP 

investigated adding a similar policy for the summer season, staff concluded this was already 

captured by the loss of load expectation (LOLE) study and closed the initiative. The LOLE study 

does not address the fact that correlated outages in conventional resources are not accounted 

for in accreditation and, as a result, the reliability cost of these outages will continue to be 

socialized to the region. 

SPP does not have an effective mechanism to measure and incorporate performance in resource 

availability accreditation. The current accreditation methodology for conventional resources 

does not consider resource performance beyond an operational test. While SPP and 

stakeholders have submitted a new effective load carrying capability (ELCC) accreditation 

methodology for variable energy resources and a performance-based accreditation (PBA) 

methodology for conventional resources that consider historical performance, the latter does 

not consider out of management control forced outages, maintenance, or planned outages. As 

such, a gap between accreditation and expected availability will likely remain if the proposed 

accreditation processes go into effect without further changes, including weighing conventional 

resource performance during critical periods. 

Additionally, the MMU does not believe the subset of outages included, combined with 

averaging performance over a seven-year period, will fully incentivize resources to address 

shortfalls in their performance. Moreover, it will not reward resources that have taken steps to 

improve their performance, reducing the incentive for LREs to make substantial investments to 

improve availability. While the planning reserve margin (PRM) will account for this gap, 

socializing resource adequacy through the PRM is inefficient and may translate into greater 

reliability risks long-term. As such, the MMU continues to recommend that SPP and stakeholders 

develop availability incentives to enhance resource performance and reliability. 

Addressing resource adequacy is perhaps the most important lesson from the winter storms 

over the last several years. The SPP system was fortunate to have significant imports from MISO, 

PJM, and others; particularly in 2021 and 2024. SPP should not depend on these systems in 

future planning models, as a wider regional cold snap could limit imports. In December 2022 

and January 2024, SPP avoided severe outcomes largely because of the availability of wind. 

Effectively improving resource adequacy will require seasonal variation, effective measurement 

of performance, accurate measurements and commensurate incentives for performance 

4 See ER24-2953 

State of the Market 2024 

11 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Executive summary 

improvements (or declines), and must go beyond an expectation of availability before the 

season and incentivize real-time availability. The MMU has engaged and will continue to engage 

in the SPP stakeholder processes to help promote improved resource adequacy. 

1.7  RECOMMENDATIONS 

One of the primary responsibilities of a market monitoring unit is evaluating market rules and 

design features, to improve market efficiency and effectiveness. When issues are identified 

within the market, one of the ways to correct them is to make recommendations on market 

enhancements. These recommendations are documented in more detail in Chapter 7. The MMU 

is making four new recommendations for 2024, as well as highlighting some existing 

recommendations in an effort to promote the need for these issues to be addressed. 

  NEW RECOMMENDATIONS FOR 2024 

2024.1  Market-based incentive to ensure daily availability of adequate accredited capacity to 

meet daily load 

From 2021 to 2024, the SPP region has experienced four major winter weather events. During 

those events, SPP spent a total of 335 hours in conservative operations, 44 hours in an Energy 

Emergency Alert (EEA) level 1, 43 hours in EEA level 2, and 9 hours in EEA level 3.  

SPP has made a number of tariff revisions since 2021 to address reliability, including establishing 

a winter season resource adequacy requirement and a separate winter season planning reserve 

margin, which will be implemented for the 2026 winter season. SPP has also filed revisions to 

attachment AA of the SPP Open Access Transmission Tariff (Tariff) with the Federal Energy 

Regulatory Commission (FERC) to better align resource accreditation with past performance. In 

addition to tariff revisions, the North American Electric Reliability Corporation (NERC) has made 

updates to their reliability requirements. In response to the new NERC standards and the more 

frequent severe weather events, resource owners have made material improvements to try to 

ensure increased availability and reliability of their resources. 

Since 2021, there has been a marginal increase in overall resource performance, particularly 

during severe weather events, but the overall reliability in the region remains challenged due to 

load growth. Additionally, there is no immediate incentive through the resource adequacy 

construct for a resource to be available to serve load on any given day. Therefore, to ensure 

reliability and an efficient, competitive market, the MMU recommends the development of a 

market incentive for resources to meet regional load obligations on a daily basis. This incentive 

should focus on accredited resources but be structured to benefit any resource when there is a 

shortage of available accredited resources. The MMU prefers a market-based solution to capture 

State of the Market 2024 

12 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Executive summary 

the efficiency gains from a competitive market. Alternatively, a non-market solution could be 

used to incentivize capacity to be available to serve load obligations on a daily basis. A non-

market solution would need to mirror the accreditation process with an ex-post review of a load 

responsible entity’s (LRE) actual load compared to pre-season forecast, and the availability of 

accredited resources across the season with emphasis on the tightest periods. 

2024.2 Sufficiency valuation curve transparency 

A critical piece of the supply adequacy process is an effective incentive structure to ensure LREs 

meet their individual Resource Adequacy Requirements (RARs). The MMU, in collaboration with 

SPP and stakeholders, designed the Sufficiency Valuation Curve (SVC) as an alternative to the 

standard deficiency payment when a deficiency can be attributed to a sudden and significant 

change in the Planning Reserve Margin (PRM). While the standard deficiency payment charges a 

multiple of the Cost of New Entry (CONE) to incentivize new entry, the SVC instead estimates the 

value of excess accredited capacity in the region based on CONE, net CONE, and the amount of 

total accredited capacity available to the region relative to the region’s non-coincident peak 

load and PRM. Payments collected via the SVC are distributed to excess accredited capacity as 

compensation for standing in for deficient LREs. 

The MMU is concerned that the effectiveness of the SVC as an incentive for resource adequacy 

is at risk due to a lack of transparency and a need for process improvements. As such, we first 

recommend that SPP recalculate the value of net CONE annually. In addition, this value and the 

clearing price of the SVC should be published publicly once available to provide transparent and 

symmetric information to all LREs and market participants in order to allow them to make more 

informed decisions related to capacity in the future. 

2024.3 Identification of affiliates in the market 

The lack of transparency of the affiliates in the SPP markets leads to less efficient market 

surveillance and challenges in detecting potential gaming and market manipulation. The MMU 

recommends SPP implement a design similar to the current WEIS Market design in which a 

market participant must identify all of its affiliates within the market at the time of registration. 

Existing participants must timely update their registration to reflect current affiliations. The MMU 

recommends the following definition for affiliate.  

Affiliate: With respect to a corporation, partnership or other entity, each such other corporation, 

partnership or other entity that directly or indirectly, through one or more intermediaries, controls, 

is controlled by, or is under common control with, such corporation, partnership or other entity as 

defined under 18 CFR 35.36(a)(9). 

State of the Market 2024 

13 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

2024.4 Cost of New Entry (CONE) update 

Executive summary 

SPP's gross Cost of New Entry (CONE) study assumptions and the associated value, currently set 

at $85.61/kW-year, are outdated and require reevaluation. CONE is used to indicate the current, 

annualized, capital cost of a new hypothetical resource and estimate the breakeven revenue 

required for investment. In SPP's construct, the CONE value is used to determine the payment 

that a deficient load responsible entity is charged if they lack sufficient accredited capacity to 

cover their forecasted peak load and planning reserve margin. The existing value is based on 

assumptions from the Energy Information Agency's 2013 report, which became effective in 2018 

with the approval of SPP's Tariff Attachment AA by FERC in Docket ER18-1268-000. No 

adjustments have been made since inception to either the CONE values or the study 

assumptions for the process. 

Compared to other RTOs and ISOs, SPP's gross CONE value stands as an outlier, with a 

significantly lower value than other markets, and a comprehensive update incorporating modern 

assumptions is anticipated to result in a sizable increase. The current resource adequacy 

workplan does not currently include a scheduled reevaluation of the CONE value. SPP has 

indicated plans to initiate stakeholder discussions in time for a CONE update in 2028, which 

provides sufficient time to represent an opportunity for revamping the study process.  

To align with best practices and ensure market efficiency, SPP should incorporate into its 

resource advisory workplan a plan to reevaluate its CONE value and processes (such as selection 

of the reference technology type, frequency of CONE update, inflation assumptions, cost of 

capital percentage, transparency rules, etc.) and ensure that the CONE value is as accurate as 

practicable going forward. 

  PREVIOUS RECOMMENDATIONS 

The MMU has provided recommendations to improve market design in each of our previous 

annual state of the market reports since the launch of the Integrated Marketplace in 2014. While 

SPP and its stakeholders have found ways to effectively address some of the MMU’s concerns, 

there are still many outstanding recommendations. The MMU would prefer to see more action 

with regard to these outstanding recommendations. Discussion of previous recommendations 

and their status can be found in Chapter 7.

  CLOSED RECOMMENDATIONS 

The following recommendations from previous reports were closed in 2024. 

2022.4 Improve congestion hedging mechanism to enhance equity 

Revision request 591 - HITT M1 congestion hedging improvements - was approved by FERC in 

July 2024. SPP submitted a filing in December 2024 setting the effective date of the Tariff 

revisions for January 2, 2025. While the revision request only addresses roughly half of the 

available congestion hedges in SPP, the MMU believes it is a step in the right direction to 

obtaining better equity among members’ abilities to hedge congestion risks. The MMU 
considers this recommendation closed at this time.5 

2018.5  Improve regulation mileage price formation 

In the 2018 annual report, the MMU recommended SPP staff review the performance of 

regulation mileage and develop potential approaches to improve regulation mileage price 

formation. In addition, based on independent analysis, the MMU recommended SPP staff 

address two inefficiencies; one in the calculation of the factor used to determine average 
expected mileage, and one in the calculation of the clearing price for mileage.6 SPP 

implemented changes in June 2024 which address the MMU recommendations. The MMU 

considers this recommendation closed at this time.

hedging mechanisms to enhance equity

2  LOAD AND RESOURCES 

Load and resources 

This chapter reviews load and resources in the SPP market for 2024. Key points from this chapter 

include: 

•  Monthly average system energy consumption was up 3% compared to 2023 levels. Most 

of this increase can be attributed to load growth rather than factors such as weather. 

• 

In 2024, 1,083 MW of wind generation capacity was added to the market. Wind 

generation capacity now accounts for 34% of installed nameplate capacity in the SPP 

market. 

•  As of December 31, 2024, there were 121 demand response resources in the SPP market, 

representing 968 MW of nameplate capacity. This represents a net loss of 11 resources 

compared to the end of 2023, but a net gain of 175 MW of nameplate capacity. In 2024, 

53 dispatchable demand response resources retired, representing 125 MW of nameplate 

capacity. In 2024, 42 dispatchable demand response resources were added to the 

market, representing a total of 259 MW of nameplate capacity 

•  The generation interconnection queue has nearly 128,000 MW of requested projects at 

the end of 2024. Of the queue, about 30,000 MW are wind resources, 44,000 MW are 

solar resources, 25,000 MW are battery/storage resources, with the remainder hybrid or 

gas simple-cycle resources. 

•  Wind generation represented the largest portion of total energy produced at 38% of the 

total. Coal generation was next highest at 25% of the total.  

•  SPP remained a net exporter for 2024 with an hourly average of 156 MW, down from 418 

MW in 2023.  

•  Net market-to-market payments from MISO to SPP were $112 million, 22% higher than 

2023 payments of $93 million.  

•  Cleared virtual energy bids and offers as a percentage of load for 2024 was 29%, up from 

26% in 2023. 

•  Average profit per cleared virtual megawatt after fees was $1.25/MW, up 13% from 2023.  

2.1  THE INTEGRATED MARKETPLACE 

SPP is a Regional Transmission Organization (RTO) authorized by the Federal Energy Regulatory 

Commission (FERC) to ensure reliable power supplies, adequate transmission infrastructure, and 

State of the Market 2024 

17 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

competitive wholesale electricity prices. FERC granted RTO status to SPP in 2004. SPP provides 

many services to its members, including reliability coordination, tariff administration, regional 

scheduling, reserve sharing, transmission expansion planning, wholesale electricity market 

operations, and training. This report focuses on the 2024 calendar year of the SPP wholesale 

electricity market referred to as the Integrated Marketplace, which started on March 1, 2014. 

The Integrated Marketplace has transmission congestion rights, a day-ahead market with virtual 

trading, a reliability unit commitment process, a real-time balancing market, and a price-based 

operating reserves market. SPP simultaneously put into operation a single balancing authority as 

part of the implementation of the Integrated Marketplace. The primary benefit of a day-ahead 

market is improved efficiency of daily resource commitments. Another benefit of this market 

includes the joint optimization of the available capacity for energy and operating reserves. 

SPP MARKET FOOTPRINT 

The SPP market footprint is located in the westernmost portion of the Eastern Interconnection, 

with Midcontinent ISO (MISO) to the east, Electric Reliability Council of Texas (ERCOT) to the 

south, and the Western Interconnection to the west. Figure 2–1 shows the current operating 

regions of the nine RTO/ISO markets in the United States and Canada, as well as a more detailed 

view of the SPP footprint. The SPP market also has connections with other non-RTO/ISO areas 

such as Saskatchewan Power Corporation, Associated Electric Cooperative, and Southwestern 
Power Administration.7  

7 Southwestern Power Administration belongs to the SPP RTO, Reliability Coordinator (RC), and Reserve 
Sharing Group (RSG) footprints. Associated Electric Cooperative belongs to the SPP RSG.

SPP market participants can be divided into several categories: regulated investor-owned 

utilities, electric cooperatives, municipal utilities, federal and state agencies, independent power 

producers, and financial-only market participants that do not own physical assets. Figure 2–2

Marketplace.

Figure 2–3  Nameplate capacity by market participant type

participants (68%) representing a smaller portion (21%) of total nameplate capacity.

2.2  ELECTRICITY DEMAND  

  SYSTEM PEAK DEMAND 

Load and resources 

One way to evaluate load is to review peak system demand statistics over an extended period. 

The market footprint has changed over time as participants have been added to or withdrawn 

from the market. The peak demand values reviewed in this section are coincident peaks, 

calculated out of total generation dispatch across the entire market footprint that occurred 

during a specific real-time market five-minute interval. The peak experienced during a particular 

year or season is affected by events such as unusually hot or cold weather, daily and seasonal 

load patterns, and economic growth and change. 

Figure 2–4 shows a month-by-month comparison of five-minute demand for the last three 

years. 

Figure 2–4  Monthly peak system demand 

In 2024, nine of twelve months exceeded the peak from 2023. The SPP system five-minute 

interval peak demand in 2024 was 54,719 MW, which occurred on July 15 at the five-minute 

interval beginning at 3:50 PM. This was below the all-time peak set in 2023 of 56,139 MW. 

Figure 2–5 shows the seasonal peak for five-minute demand for the last three years. Winter 

comprises January, February, and October through December, while Summer comprises March 

through September. 

State of the Market 2024 

21 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Figure 2–5  Seasonal peak system demand 

Load and resources 

Winter peak system demand for 2024 was 46,821 MW, which was 13% above 2023, while 

summer peak system demand for 2024 was 54,719 MW, 2.5% below 2023. The winter peak was 

set during the winter weather event in late January and the summer peak was set in late July.

aggregate level were between 96% and 99% of the actual real-time load in 2024. Accurate

The five largest entities – four investor-owned utilities and one large cooperative – account for 

nearly 64% of energy consumed in the market. This concentration is understandable as SPP’s 

market is primarily composed of vertically integrated investor-owned utilities, which tend to be 

large. Overall, the total system energy usage in 2024 was 3% above the 2023 level. 

Figure 2–8 shows the monthly system energy consumption in thousands of gigawatt-hours.  

Figure 2–8  System energy consumption 

For the year, monthly average energy consumption was up 3% compared to 2023. The monthly 

average was higher in six months of 2024 compared to 2023, and nine months of 2024 were 

higher than 2022. 

Figure 2–9 depicts load duration curves from 2022 to 2024. These load duration curves display 

hourly loads from the highest to the lowest for each year. 

State of the Market 2024 

25

Figure 2–9 

Load duration curve 

Load and resources 

In 2024, the maximum hourly average load was 52,279 MW, which was down 2% from 2023. The 

minimum hourly load for 2024 was 22,946 MW, which was 7% above 2023. Comparing annual 

load duration curves shows differentiation between cases of extreme loading events and more 

general increases in system demand. If only the extremes are higher or lower than the previous 

year, then short-term loading events are likely the reason. If the entire curve is higher across the 

entire range, this is more indicative of increased system demand. For 2024, the curve remains 

slightly above 2023 for most of the curve, indicating an overall increase in system demand. 

  HEATING AND COOLING DEGREE DAYS 

Changes in weather patterns from year-to-year have a significant impact on electricity demand. 

One way to evaluate this impact is to calculate heating degree days (HDD) and cooling degree 

days (CDD). These values can then be used to estimate the impact of actual weather conditions 
on energy consumption, compared to normal weather patterns.8 Regression analysis has shown 

8 To determine heating degree days and cooling degree days for the SPP footprint, several representative 
locations are used in the calculation. These locations include Shreveport LA, Lubbock TX, Oklahoma City 
OK, Amarillo TX, Kansas City, MO, Hays, KS, Omaha NE, North Platte NE, Sioux Falls SD, Rapid City SD, 
Grand Forks ND, and Williston/Stanley ND. The base temperature separating heating and cooling periods 
is 65 degrees Fahrenheit. If the average temperature of a day at a location is 75 degrees Fahrenheit, there 
would be 10 (=75–65) cooling degree days at that location. If a day’s average temperature is 50 degrees 
Fahrenheit, there would be 15 (=65–50) heating degree days at that location. Using statistical tools, the 
daily estimated load impact of a single cooling degree day is just over four times higher than the impact of 
a single heating degree day. This is in part because more electric is used for cooling than electric heating. 
So, in order to show the actual impact of degree days, cooling degree days are multiplied by 4.2 in Figure 
2–10. 

State of the Market 2024 

26 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

that a cooling degree has about 4.2 times the impact of a heating degree on load, so cooling 

degree days are multiplied by 4.2 in the chart below. 

Figure 2–10 shows monthly heating and cooling degree days’ impact over the last three years 

compared to the average hourly load. 

Figure 2–10  Heating and cooling degree days 

As shown in the chart, cooling degree days are more prevalent in the higher load months of 

May through September, whereas heating degree days are more prevalent in the other months. 

Total degree day impact in 2024 was down 1% from 2023. Average hourly load up 3% in 2024 

compared to 2023. The fact that average hourly load was up and degree day impact was down 

indicates that the increase in average hourly load can be primarily attributed to load growth in 

2024, as opposed to weather impacts. 

Figure 2–11 and Figure 2–12 show cooling degree days and heating degree days for the past 
three years compared to a normal year.9 

9 The 30-year normal temperatures are from the 1991-2020 U.S. Climate Normals product from the 
National Oceanic and Atmospheric Association (NOAA) at https://www.ncei.noaa.gov/access/us-climate-
normals/.  

State of the Market 2024 

27 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

Figure 2–11  Cooling degree days compared with a normal year 

Figure 2–12  Heating degree days compared with a normal year 

The figures indicate loads are influenced by cooling demand in the late spring and summer 

months, whereas late fall and winter loads are, to a lesser degree, influenced by heating 

demand. Moreover, the figures show that cooling degree days in 2024 were well above the 30-

year average in all “cooling months” (April through October). Heating degree days in “heating 

months” (January through March, November, and December) were all at or below the 30-year 

average in all months, expect for January, indicating warmer than average temperatures.

 Total installed nameplate generation capacity in the SPP Integrated Marketplace was 102,792 
MW at the end of 2024, representing an increase of 2.3% (or 2,352 MW) from 2023.10  

Wind had the highest share of total nameplate capacity at 34% of total nameplate generation. 

When both types of natural gas resources are combined, natural gas-fired installed generation 

capacity still represents the largest share of generation capacity in the SPP market at 35% (gas 

simple-cycle at 22% and gas combined-cycle at 13%) of nameplate capacity, with coal being the 

third largest type at 21%. 

Although not yet a significant portion of nameplate capacity, SPP has five registered market 

storage resources, representing 142 MW of capacity. While currently only a small portion of total 

nameplate capacity, there are a significant number of market storage resources represented in 

the generation interconnection queue, as discussed in Figure 2–18. 

Figure 2–14 illustrates that certain segments of the SPP generation fleet are aging.  

10 The change in total generation capacity from year to year includes additions, retirements, fuel type

Southwest Power Pool, Inc.

Figure 2–14  Capacity by age of resource 

Load and resources 

As of the end of 2024, 42% of SPP’s generation fleet is more than 30 years old. In particular, 86% 

of coal capacity and 43% of gas capacity is older than 30 years. According to the U.S. Energy 

Information Administration (EIA), the national average retirement age of coal-fired generation in 
2024 was 50 years.11 Aside from the resources that joined SPP from Nebraska in 2009 and the 
Integrated System12 in 2015, the largest source of new capacity in the SPP footprint over the last 

10 years has been wind capacity. 

Figure 2–15 shows the annual trend of capacity additions and retirements over the past three 

years. 

11 Through December 2024. See https://www.eia.gov/electricity/data/eia860M/.  
12 Market participants added as part of the Integrated System are Western Area Power Administration –

30 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

Figure 2–15  Capacity additions and retirements by year 

Almost all the coal and gas capacity retired since 2016 have been plants that started operation 

in the 1950s and 1960s. Of the 409 MW of retired capacity in 2024, 387 MW came from gas, and 

22 MW from dispatchable demand response resources. 

Total nameplate capacity additions were 2,759 MW in 2024. For capacity additions, wind 

generation has accounted for 61% of the additions over the last three years. Only 1,083 MW of 

wind generation was added in 2024, down from 3,200 MW in 2021 (not shown), 1,579 MW in 

2022, and 1,705 MW in 2023. This year saw the addition of 402 MW of solar resources, up from 

the 225 MW that were added in 2023. Also of note, an additional 284 MW of nameplate capacity 

of dispatchable demand response resources was added to the market in 2024, bringing the total 

nameplate capacity of these resources to 970 MW and 130 MW of nameplate capacity of market 

storage resources was added in 2024, for a total of 142 MW of nameplate capacity. This 

indicates that the solar and market storage resources in the interconnection process are slowly 

starting to work their way through and become closer to operational status. Considering the 
2,759 MW in capacity additions along with 409 MW of retirements, 2,350 MW13 of net 

generating capacity was added to the SPP market in 2024. 

  AGGREGATE SUPPLY 

Figure 2–16 shows the total SPP aggregate real-time generation supply curves14 by offer price, 

peak demand, and average demand for the summers of 2022 to 2024, while Figure 2–17 shows 

13 This figure varies from the growth in nameplate capacity of 2,352 MW shown in Figure 2-13, as each year 
many resources undergo capacity rating changes not reflected in the additions and retirements. 
14 The real-time generation supply curves of the seasonal peak demand dates. 

State of the Market 2024 

31 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

the same data for the winter months. Resources in outage status were excluded from the supply 

curve. To calculate the supply curves, the peak day for each season was used for each analysis 

year. The aggregate generation supply curves were calculated by using the real-time offers of 

non-wind resources and wind forecast data for wind resources.  

Figure 2–16  Aggregate supply curve, peak summer day 

Total aggregate real-time generation supply for summer 2024 was 87,062 MW, compared to 

78,157 MW for summer 2023, an increase of 11.4% which was due to significant wind generation 
difference in these two peak dates.15 The system peak demand in 2024 of 54,719 MW was a 

2.5% decrease from 2023, However, the average demand had a 3.2% increase from 2023 to 

2024. Based on the heating and cooling degree days analysis in Section 2.2.3, the SPP market 

footprint had slightly higher cooling degree days in June and July 2024 compared to 2023, 

which resulted in higher average demand in the summer months as compared to the previous 

year. 

Also evident is the approximately 15.3 GW gap between this maximum supply and the total 

installed nameplate generation capacity on the peak summer day. This is primarily a result of the 

difference between the wind forecast and installed capacity of wind resources (approximately 10 

GW), resources reporting on outage (approximately 5 GW), and reduced summer capacity due 

to high ambient temperatures (less than 0.3 GW).  

15 On hourly average, wind resources offered about 8.8 GW on the 2023 peak demand date, while about 15 
GW was offered on the 2024 peak demand date. 

State of the Market 2024 

32 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

Figure 2–17  Aggregate supply curve, peak winter day 

The total aggregate real-time generation supply for winter 2024 was 74,230 MW, compared to 

70,044 MW for winter 2023, a 6% increase. The system winter peak demand of 46,821 MW for 

2024 was 15% higher than 2023. The high peak demand in 2024 was attributed to some unusally 

cold weather in January 2024.  

The section of the offer curve below $0/MWh is mostly due to wind and solar energy and can 

vary between 1,000 MW and 20,000 MW, based on wind and solar availability. Negative offers 

typically reflect opportunity costs associated with state and federal tax incentives. The sharp 

uptick in price at the top of the supply curves represents the transition from natural gas units to 

oil units.  

Comparing the supply curves of 2022 with 2023 and 2024, prices shift at around 40 GW for both 

the summer and winter curves in 2022, which likely represents the transition from coal units to 

natural gas units at high natural gas prices in 2022. 

  GENERATION INTERCONNECTION 

SPP is responsible for performing engineering studies to determine if the interconnection of 

new generation within the SPP footprint is feasible, and to identify any transmission 

development that would be necessary to facilitate the proposed generation. The generation 

interconnection process involves a cluster study methodology allowing participants several 
windows to submit requests for evaluation.16  

16 See Guidelines for Generator Interconnection Requests to SPP’s Transmission System 

State of the Market 2024 

33 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc.

As the chart above shows, at the end of 2024, generation totaling just over 23 GW has an

with wind representing 11 GW of this generation. It is important to note that of the on-schedule 

projects, 49% are for wind resources, 39% are for solar resources, and 11% are for 

battery/storage resources. However, when looking at the entire generation interconnection 

queue, 34% of the projects are for solar resources, 24% are for wind resources, 20% are for 

battery/storage resources, and 11% each for hybrid and gas resources. Although solar resources 

make up a larger portion of the entire queue, the fact that wind resources represent a larger 

portion of the on-schedule projects in the queue indicates that wind resources will still be the 

predominant type of resource added to the market in the near future. Also note that generation 

can still be added or removed from the on-schedule project list, even in the current year. 

However, there is more surety to the levels of generation scheduled to go into production closer 

to the current year, and additions and deletions to on-schedule projects are more typical in 

future years. 

2.4  GENERATION 

  GENERATION BY TECHNOLOGY 

An analysis of generation by technology type used in the SPP Integrated Marketplace is useful in 

understanding pricing and reliability, as well as the potential impact of environmental and 

additional regulatory requirements on resources in the SPP system. Information on fuel and 

technology types and fleet characteristics is also useful in understanding market dynamics 

regarding congestion management, price volatility, and overall market efficiency. 

State of the Market 2024 

35 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

Figure 2–20 depicts annual generation percentages in the SPP real-time market by technology 

type for the years 2014 through 2024.  

Figure 2–20  Generation by technology type, real-time, annual 

Some of the annual fluctuations in generation by technology type shares are driven by the 

relative difference in primary fuel prices, namely natural gas versus coal. The long-term trend for 

coal-fired generation had been relatively flat prior to 2014 (not shown on chart above) but has 

been in a steady decline ever since. This is primarily due to low gas prices from 2015 to 2020, 

causing efficient gas generation to displace some coal generation, as can be seen in the higher 

generation from combined-cycle gas plants. Higher gas prices in 2021 and 2022 led to a 

temporary reversal of this trend. During that period, coal generation became more economic 

than many gas resources, resulting in an increase in the percentage of total generation by coal 

to 36% year-over-year. With gas prices returning to previous levels, coal’s share of total 

generation has declined by 11 percentage points. 

Wind generation’s share rose to 37.9% in 2024, following the recent decline to 36.6% in 2023. 

The drop in wind generation in 2024 was primarily attributed to the increase in increased 

capacity, as shown in Figure 2–28. With the return of lower gas prices, generation from gas units 

such as gas turbines and gas steam turbines increased back to pre-2020 levels; gas, combined-

cycle at 17% of total generation and gas, simple-cycle at 12%. 

Retirement of older coal generation, environmental limits, and competition from renewable and 

natural gas technologies are some of the factors that will continue to put pressure on coal 

generation levels. In addition, renewable generation is expected to continue to increase in the 

years ahead.

Wind generation as a percentage of total generation is generally lowest in the summer months 

at levels around 27%. The highest wind generation months are typically during spring and fall. 

During these seasons, monthly wind generation levels can approach, or even exceed, 50% of 

total generation. In 2024, wind reached a high of 53% in April. Additionally, wind generation 

outpaced coal in most months, excluding January, July, and August. 

One method commonly used to assess price trends and relative efficiency in electricity markets 

originating from non-fuel costs is the implied heat rate. The implied heat rate is calculated by 

dividing the electricity price, net of a representative value for variable operations and 
maintenance (VOM) costs, by the fuel (gas) price.18 For a gas generator, the implied heat rate 

serves as a “break-even” point for profitability such that a unit producing output with an 

operating (actual) heat rate below the implied heat rate would be earning profits, given market 

prices for electricity and gas. If the price of natural gas was $3/MMBtu, and the electricity price 

was $24/MWh, the implied heat rate would be (24/3) = 8 MMBtu/MWh (8,000 Btu/kWh). This 

implied heat rate shows the relative efficiency required of a generator to convert gas to 

electricity and cover the variable costs of production, given market prices. 

Figure 2–22 shows the monthly implied heat rate using real-time electricity prices for 2022 to 

2024, along with an annual average for those years.  

18 For the implied heat rate calculation, natural gas units are assumed to be on the margin and accordingly, 
gas prices are taken as the relevant fuel cost. Emission costs are ignored in fuel cost as they rarely apply in 
the SPP market. 

State of the Market 2024 

37 

 
 
 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Figure 2–22  Implied heat rate 

Load and resources 

As shown above, the implied heat rate for 2024 was significantly above that of 2022 and all of 

2023, except for June and December. 2024 averaged of over 14,400 Btu/KWh, compared to a 

2023 average of over 12,600 Btu/KWh – an increase of 14%. With implied heat rates in the 

17,500 Btu/KWh range during July and August and the actual heat rates for most gas resources 

below this level, this would indicate, at least for the summer months, that most gas resources 

would have been profitable given the electricity and gas prices during the peak summer months. 

  GENERATION ON THE MARGIN 

The system marginal price represents the price of the next increment of generation available to 

meet the next increment of total system demand. The locational marginal price at a particular 

pricing node is the system marginal energy price plus any marginal congestion charges and 

marginal loss charges associated with that pricing node. For a generator to set the marginal 

price, the resource must be: 1) dispatchable by the market; 2) not at the resource economic 

minimum or maximum; and 3) not ramp limited. In other words, it must be able to move to 

provide the next increment of generation. 

Day-ahead generation on the margin, shown in Figure 2–23, is different from real-time in that 

the day-ahead market includes virtual transactions. The real-time market does not include virtual 

transactions and is required to adjust to unforeseeable market conditions such as unexpected 

plant and transmission outages.  

State of the Market 2024 

38 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

Figure 2–23  Generation on the margin, day-ahead 

In 2024, virtual transactions were on the margin the highest percentage of hours, at 22% in the 

day-ahead market. Gas, simple-cycle and coal resources both accounted for 22% of hours, 

followed by wind, at 21%, and lastly, gas, combined-cycle at 11% of total hours. While marginal 

virtual offers occur at all types of settlement locations, 70% of marginal virtual offers are at 

resource settlement locations, with a significant amount of that activity at wind generation 

resource locations.  

Figure 2–24 illustrates the frequency with which different technology types were marginal and 

price setting in the real-time market.  

Figure 2–24  Generation on the margin, real-time 

State of the Market 2024 

39 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

After dropping to 30% in 2023, wind generation on the margin grew to 37% in 2024. With the 

growing amount of dispatchable wind generation and an overall share of 34% of total 

nameplate capacity, wind generation is increasingly becoming the marginal technology a higher 

percentage of the time. At the end of 2024, 98% of nameplate wind capacity was dispatchable, 

compared to 97% at the end of 2023. At the beginning of the Integrated Marketplace in March 

2014, just 27% of nameplate wind capacity was dispatchable.  

The most significant difference between day-ahead and real-time fuel on the margin is the 

absence of virtual offers in the real-time market. From the day-ahead to the real-time market, 

wind resources on the margin increased from 21% to 37% of all intervals (16 percentage points), 

while gas, simple-cycle and combined-cycle increased by five and six percentage points, 

respectively. Coal resources on the margin dropped by five percentage points from the day-

ahead to the real-time.  

On a monthly basis, intervals with coal generation on the margin are typically lower in the spring 

and fall months, offset by wind resources acting as base load units. This results in coal and gas-

fired units cycling more often. Increased wind generation also affects prices to some extent 

every month of the year. The higher wind generation on the margin values in the spring and fall 

are as expected given that these periods are the windiest times of the year, as well as the lowest 

demand periods in the SPP footprint.

2.6  GROWING IMPACT OF WIND GENERATION CAPACITY 

  WIND CAPACITY AND GENERATION 

The SPP region has a high potential for wind generation given wind patterns in many areas of 

the footprint. Federal incentives and state renewable portfolio standards and incentives are 

additional factors that have resulted in significant investment of wind generation capacity in the 

SPP footprint during the last several years. 

One of the primary drivers for the amount of wind generation available in the market footprint 

is, not surprisingly, the amount of wind that is blowing at any point in time. Figure 2–27 shows 

the average monthly wind speed at three locations located in the portion of the footprint with 

an abundance of wind resources – Amarillo, TX; Lubbock, TX; and Hays, KS. 

Figure 2–27  Wind speed 

As shown above, the 2024 average wind speed at those locations was 11.7 mph, down just 

slightly from 11.8 mph in 2023. The first four months of 2024 had wind speeds significantly 

lower than 2023, while the rest of 2024 was near 2023 speeds. 

Figure 2–28 depicts nameplate capacity and average monthly generation of SPP wind facilities 

by year since 2014.

Total registered wind nameplate capacity at the end of 2024 was 34,808 MW, an increase of 

nearly 1,083 MW from 2023. At the end of 2024, 98% of all nameplate wind capacity was 

dispatchable, while just 2% was non-dispatchable. After wind generation dropped from 2022 to 

2023, the average monthly wind generation output increased by 7% in 2024 to 110,400 GWh.  

Consistent with previous years, wind generation fluctuated seasonally with summer being the 

low wind season and spring and fall being the high wind seasons. Wind also conformed to 

previous trends where production tended to be lower during on-peak hours than off-peak. 

Finally, higher levels of wind generation tended to coincide with the morning ramp periods.

remained around a 10 percentage point difference, illustrates the disconnect in the amount of 

wind in the real-time market compared to the cleared wind in the day-ahead market.

45 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

Figure 2–32  Average demand, net demand, and wind generation by hour of day 

Wind generation typically produces more energy when demand is lowest and less when demand 

is highest. Wind generation is at the highest levels in the overnight hours while demand is low. 

In the morning hours, wind generation decreases while load increases. To navigate these 

transitional hours, available rampable capacity is necessary. During these transitional hours, 
ramp scarcity is more likely.20 While the marginal costs of wind generation are very low and 

result in lower clearing prices, it is important to pair wind generation with capacity that is 

available to ramp up. 

While Figure 2–32 shows the yearly average demand, wind, and net demand, there are seasonal 

differences as well. For instance, in the summer, wind generation is lower than during other 

times of the year, and loads are higher. Thus, the effect on net demand is smaller. However, in 

spring and fall, loads are lower, and wind can have a significant effect on net demand. 

  WIND INTEGRATION 

Wind integration brings low marginal cost generation to the SPP region but does not count for 

much accredited capacity. There are a number of operational challenges in dealing with 

substantial wind capacity. For instance, wind energy output varies by season and time of day. 

This variability is estimated to be about four times more than load when measured on an hour-

to-hour basis. Moreover, wind is counter-cyclical to load. As load increases (both seasonally and 

daily), wind production typically declines. The increasing magnitude of wind capacity additions, 

along with the locational concentration, volatility, and variablitity of wind, can create challenges 

for grid operators with regard to managing transmission congestion and resolution of ramping 

20 This is discussed in Section 3.2.5.  

State of the Market 2024 

46 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

constraints. Wind forecasting errors can lead to price spikes while the under-clearing of wind in 

the day-ahead market is a leading cause of day-ahead and real-time price divergence.  

Figure 2–33 illustrates average hourly curtailments for wind resources over the past three years. 

In real-time, there are two sources of wind curtailments: automated market software and 

manual. Automated market software curtailments occur when wind resources are dispatched 

down by the market system primarily to mitigate transmission constraints, while manual 

curtailments occur when the SPP reliability coordinator issues an out-of-merit-energy (OOME) 

instruction to manage reliability issues that cannot be handled through re-dispatch instructions. 

Figure 2–33  Curtailments for wind resources 

From 2019 to 2024, total average hourly curtailments increased substantially from 137 MW to 

1,483 MW. 2023 represented the first year in the SPP market that curtailments decreased, 

however, curtailments rebounded in 2024 to a record high. This occurred primarily because of 

higher wind generation output potential. In 2024, manual curtailments doubled as a percentage 

of total curtailments from 1% to 2% because of increased congestion and high wind generation, 

which required action from operators to provide relief along certain constraints. Three historic 

factors associated with the high level of wind curtailments in the SPP market include the large 

amount of wind generation relative to the regional transmission buildout, the counter-cyclical 

nature of wind curtailments compared to load, and the conversion of previously non-

dispatchable wind resources to dispatchable wind resources. Although two of these factors were 

partially mitigated in 2023 (lower wind generation and more transmission expansion), in 2024, 

these factors accelerated wind curtailments to a new record. 

Figure 2–34 displays average hourly automated market software wind curtailments in 2024. 

Wind curtailments tend to occur in the early-morning and late-night hours when wind 

State of the Market 2024 

47 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

generation is higher, and load is lower. These two factors magnify each other – by increasing 

congestion – leading to a higher level of wind curtailments.

2.7  SEAMS 

  EXPORTS AND IMPORTS 

The SPP Integrated Marketplace has greater than 6,000 MW of AC interties with MISO to the 

east, 720 MW of DC ties to ERCOT to the south, and over 1,000 MW of DC ties to the Western 

Interconnection to the west. Additionally, SPP has over 1,500 MW of interties with the 

Southwestern Power Administration (SPA) in Arkansas, Missouri, and Oklahoma, and over 5,000 

MW of AC interties with the Associated Electric Cooperative (AECI) in Oklahoma and Missouri.

Since 2016, SPP has been a net exporter. The annual trend has been for net exports has been for

show the data for the five most directly connected interfaces in real-time, namely ERCOT

wind production tend to drive exports to ERCOT. However, ERCOT capacity additions and 

relatively low-cost natural gas production from the Permian region combined with SPP wind 

curtailments have put downward pressure on imports from SPP since 2022. Southwestern Power

highest during on-peak hours but is typically scheduled day-ahead. MISO interchange generally 

follows wind production, while AECI interchange is coordinated on an ad hoc basis. Interchange 

with SPA and AECI is less responsive to prices. The WEIS market has continued to grow its 

membership since its inception in February 2021. HVDC interfaces with the SPP RTO include 

Miles City, MT (MCWEST); Stegall, NE (SGE); and Sidney, NE (SCSE). Combined, they have over 

500 MW of thermal transmission capacity. The recent decreasing trend in exports to WEIS is 

similar to that of ERCOT.

  MARKET-TO-MARKET 

SPP began the market-to-market (M2M) process with MISO in March 2015 as part of a FERC 
requirement that also included regulation compensation and long-term congestion rights.21  

Each RTO is allocated property rights on market-to-market constraints. These are known as firm 

flow entitlements (FFE), and each RTO calculates its real-time usage, known as market flow. RTOs 

exchange money (market-to-market settlements) for redispatch based on the non-monitoring 

21 The market-to-market process, regulation compensation, and long-term congestion rights were required 
to be implemented one year after go-live of the SPP Integrated Marketplace. The market-to-market 
process under the joint operating agreement allows the monitoring RTO and non-monitoring RTO to 
efficiently manage market-to-market constraints by exchanging information (shadow prices, relief request, 
control indicators, etc.) and using the RTO with the more economic redispatch to relieve congestion. 

State of the Market 2024

Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

RTO’s22 market flow in relation to its firm flow entitlement. The non-monitoring RTO receives 

money from the monitoring RTO if its market flow is below its firm flow entitlement; it pays if 

above its firm flow entitlement. Figure 2–41 shows payments by month between SPP and MISO 

(positive is payment from MISO to SPP and negative is payment from SPP to MISO.) 

Figure 2–41  Market-to-market settlements

over $125 million in 2024 while payments from SPP to MISO totaled just over $15 million. This 

resulted in a net payment from MISO to SPP of $112 million compared to $93 million in 2023.
