2.8  VIRTUAL TRADING 

Market participants in SPP’s Integrated Marketplace may submit virtual energy offers and bids at 

any settlement location in the day-ahead market. Virtual offers represent energy sales to the 

day-ahead market that the participant needs to buy back in the real-time market. These are 

referred to as “increment offers,” which are like generation. Virtual bids represent energy 

purchases in the day-ahead market that the participant needs to sell back in the real-time 

market. These are referred to as “decrement bids,” which are like load. The value of virtual 

trading lies in its potential to converge day-ahead and real-time market prices and improve day-

ahead unit commitment decisions. 

For virtual transactions to converge prices, there must be sufficient competition in virtual 

trading; transparency in day-ahead market, reliability unit commitment, and real-time market 

operating practices; and predictability of market events. Since the market began in 2014, there 

have been increasing levels of virtual participation. Figure 2–43 displays the total volume of 

virtual transactions as a percentage of real-time market load along with wind output levels.  

State of the Market 2024 

53 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

Figure 2–43  Cleared virtual transactions as percent of real-time load 

As shown in the figure, virtual transactions averaged 29% of real-time market load in 2024, 

compared to 26% in 2023, and 29% in 2022. Cleared virtual offers as a percentage of load 

amounted to just over 17%, up from 15% in 2023, and 18% in 2022. Virtual cleared bids 

increased slightly from 11% in 2023 to 12% in 2024, while averaging 12% in 2022. Virtual bids 

typically increase during high load hours.  

Most virtual transactions occurred at wind resources in 2023 – a trend that has been increasing 

since mid-2015. Figure 2–44 illustrates the settlement location types where virtual offers clear. 

Figure 2–44  Cleared virtual offers by settlement location type 

In total, the monthly average of cleared virtual offers for 2024 was around 4,035 GW, up from 

3,425 GW in 2023. This figure shows that an average of just over 1,995 GW of virtual offers

54 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc.

Load and resources 

cleared at variable energy resources per month, during 2024.23 This is up from an average of 

nearly 1,731 GW per month in 2023. Virtual offers at wind locations remain the largest volume of 

any single location type. These large volumes highlight the possibility that market participants 

with registered wind resources may be missing financial opportunities by being under-cleared in 
the day-ahead market.24

The locations where virtual bids occur are in contrast with the locational volumes of virtual 

offers. Cleared virtual bids were primarily at load locations, followed by resources other than

down nearly 6% from over 807 GW in 2022.

23 This includes both dispatchable and non-dispatch variable energy locations. 
24 Section 4.1.3 on price convergence discusses the effects of under-cleared wind in the SPP market.  

State of the Market 2024 

55 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

Figure 2–46  Virtual offers and bids, day-ahead market 

The cleared demand bids that offered more than $30/MWh over the cleared day-ahead price, 

and the supply offers offered at less than $30/MWh under the cleared day-ahead price, are 

considered “price-insensitive.” Price-insensitive bids and offers are willing to buy/sell at a much 

higher/lower price that could lead to price divergence rather than more price-sensitive bids and 

offers that lead to price convergence between the day-ahead and real-time markets. Price-

insensitive bids and offers usually occur at locations with congestion and arbitrage against the 

day-ahead and real-time price differences. Given that price-insensitive bids and offers are likely 

to clear, these can be unprofitable if congestion around these locations does not materialize, 

leading to divergence between the markets. Compared to 2022, price-insensitive bids and price-

insensitive offers increased by 13% and just over 5% respectively. Cleared bids increased by 13% 

and cleared offers increased by 17%.

Southwest Power Pool, Inc.

In 2024, 10 months were profitable in aggregate for virtual transactions with July and August 

being the exceptions. In the 130 months since the market began, only 20 months have had a net 

loss when factoring in fees. Three factors that led to virtual trading losses in July and August 

were low wind generation, low load, and relatively higher day-ahead and real-time make-whole 

payments. Typically, virtual trading profits are higher during periods of high wind and low load 

which creates large price differences between the day-ahead and real-time markets. These price 

differences stem, in part, from under-cleared wind in the day-ahead market. This contributes to 
a higher portion of negative prices in real-time as compared to the day-ahead.25

Virtual trades profited $209 million before charges and fees in 2024, over a 12% increase from 

2023. Virtual bids can be charged distribution fees for day-ahead make-whole payments and 

virtual offers are susceptible to real-time make-whole payment distribution fees. In addition, 

both types of transactions can receive revenue neutrality uplift charge/credits and are assessed a 

per megawatt virtual fee. The average 2023 rates per megawatt for day-ahead make-whole 

payments, real-time make-whole payments, and real-time revenue neutrality uplift distributions 

are $0.14/MWh, $0.92/MWh, and $0.27/MWh, respectively. When factoring in these charges and 

credits, the net virtual trading profits for 2024 were $99.0 million. Net profits in 2024 increased 

by over 31% from $75.3 million in 2023. 

Net profits are typically small when assessed on a per megawatt basis. Figure 2–49 illustrates the 

monthly average profit per megawatt for a cleared virtual in 2023.

The chart shows that, when factoring in all fees, the average profit per megawatt in 2024 was 

$1.25 per cleared megawatt, an increase from $1.10 per cleared megawatt in 2023.  

In 2024, 125 participants transacted virtuals – an increase of eight from 2023. Figure 2–50 

illustrates each virtual participant’s virtual portfolio for the year by both net megawatts cleared 

and profits before and after adjusting for fees.  

Figure 2–50  Virtual portfolio by market participant 

Eight participants accounted for just over 46% of the virtual profits after fees, which can also be 

referred to as net profits. These participants account for roughly 29% of the transactional 

volume in the market. Virtual trading generated profits before fees for 101 participants, and 

State of the Market 2024 

59 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

profits after fees for 87 participants. The total losses after fees amounted to roughly $18.8 

million, and six entities accounted for just over 78% of that loss.  

Additionally, Figure 2–50 highlights the disparity in the trading fees paid by each market 

participant. These fees totaled over $132.7 million in 2024; they include: virtual fees (19%), real-

time revenue neutrality uplift fees (18%), day-ahead make-whole payment fees (8%), and real-

time make-whole payment fees (55%). Virtual bids are subject to virtual fees, real-time revenue 

neutrality fees, and day-ahead make-whole payment fees. Virtual offers are subject to virtual 

fees, real-time revenue neutrality fees, and real-time make-whole payment fees. Nearly three-

quarters of the total fees assessed to virtual transactions are assessed only to virtual offers.  

The discrepancy in virtual fees relates to the quantity calculation associated with payers of real-

time make-whole payments – specifically, the real-time net settlement location deviation hourly 

amount. This determinant accounted for nearly 88% of the real-time make-whole payments in 

2024, or roughly $64 million. As the name implies, the quantity applied to applicable non-virtual 

transactions includes only the incremental deviations from day-ahead, however the quantities 

assessed to virtual offers include the full virtual offer quantity.  

This calculation methodology, when combined with the larger make-whole payments normally 

associated with real-time, generally leads to higher fees associated with virtual offers when 

compared to virtual bids. In 2024, the fees associated with virtual offers amounted to $2.48 per 

megawatt compared to $1.84 per megawatt for virtual bids. This calculation methodology and 

associated incentives could be part of the reason why virtual trading offsets only part of the 

under-clearing of wind resources in the day-ahead market and should be considered as part of 

any analysis or evaluated as part of any potential solution to address price divergence. The 

market monitor will continue to evaluate these trends going forward.  

Cross-product market manipulation has been a concern in other RTO/ISO markets, and 

extensive monitoring is in place to detect potential cases in the SPP market. For example, a 

market participant may submit a money-losing virtual transaction intended to create congestion 

that benefits a transmission congestion right position for an overall net gain. Generally, this 

behavior shows up as a loss in one market, such as a virtual position, and a substantial 

associated benefit in another market, such as a transmission congestion right position. In the 

SPP market, only 10 market participants lost more than $100,000 in virtual transactions before 

fees, and 16 lost more than $100,000 in virtual transactions after fees in 2024. The market 

monitor reviews these outcomes and takes actions as needed.

60 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

3  UNIT COMMITMENT AND DISPATCH PROCESSES  

This chapter covers unit commitment and dispatch, scarcity pricing, ramp, and outages. Key 

points from this chapter include: 

•  The amount of capacity started in the day-ahead market is still down from 2022, making 

up only 57% of total commitments. As in 2023, self-commitments and reliability unit 

commitment commitments made up over one-third of started capacity.  

•  Day-ahead commitments of fast-start resources continued to grow despite SPP 

implementing fast start pricing in 2022. Make-whole payments to fast-start resources, 

however, continued to decrease from 2022.  

•  Overall participation in the day-ahead market is still strong. Participation did decrease, 

particularly among fossil fuel resources owned by load serving entities. These resources 

saw a notable uptick in the amount of capacity on outage. 

•  The region generally had an excess of capacity offered into the market compared to the 

load obligation. Two notable exceptions were during the winter storm in January, 

particularly on January 14, and again on August 26 and 27. The lack of available capacity 

combined with other factors led to SPP declaring a brief EEA on August 26.  

•  Despite generally high availability compared to the load obligation, there seems to be a 

persistent gap between the registered capacity in the region and what is offered or on a 

declared outage. The MMU will continue to investigate the cause of this gap. 

•  Additionally, 2024 saw a small but measurable number of periods where available 

conventional capacity was not sufficient (2% of hours) or nearly insufficient (6% of hours) 

to cover the region’s load obligation. During these periods, SPP would have had to rely 

on variable energy resources or imports, the output of which SPP does not have direct 

control over. 

•  Accredited capacity availability varied greatly, particularly during the winter season. 

Availability averaged 88% offered in economically and 92% offered in via emergency 

maximums in winter. For summer, these values were lower at only 84% and 87%, 

respectively.  

•  Outages still account for the main cause for accredited and unaccredited resources to be 

unavailable. In both winter and summer, there is evidence that there exists some amount 

of correlation among outages of resources sharing a common fuel type.  

State of the Market 2024 

61 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

•  Volatility in net load continues to increase, creating a greater need for energy services. 

An increase in the undispatched, forecasted resources (solar and wind) led to a greater 

need for ramp.  

•  Regulation scarcity remained flat from 2023, while ramp scarcity – both in number of 

intervals and average price – continued to increase. Most scarcity events across all 

products lasted only a single interval, though over 20% of scarcity events for both ramp 

up and uncertainty products last 6 or more intervals.  

•  The MMU continues to have concerns with the ramp and uncertainty product designs. 

Much of the cleared ramp capacity came from resources that cannot deploy the needed 

ramp in real-time; prices for both ramp and uncertainty were likely too low.  

3.1  COMMITMENT PROCESS 

The Integrated Marketplace uses centralized unit commitment to determine efficient scheduling 

and dispatch of generation resources to meet energy demand and operating reserve 

requirements. SPP employs five reliability commitment processes: 

•  multi-day reliability assessment (MDRA); 

•  day-ahead reliability unit commitment (DA RUC) process; 

• 

• 

intra-day reliability unit commitment (ID RUC) process; 

short-term intra-day reliability unit commitment (ST RUC) process; and 

•  manual commitment instructions issued by the RTO. 

Figure 3–1 shows a timeline describing when SPP executes each commitment processes. 

Figure 3–1  Commitment process timeline 

Most commitments begin in the day-ahead market. The day-ahead market attempts to commit 

sufficient capacity to meet the load that was bid into the day-ahead market. Because of 

differences between day-ahead and real-time, it is often necessary to commit additional 

capacity outside the day-ahead market. This is done through the reliability unit commitment 

(RUC) processes and manual commitments. 

State of the Market 2024 

62 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc.

major contributors to the decrease in started capacity between 2022 and 2024 came from 

resources started by the day-ahead market, while the decrease from 2023 primarily stems from a 

small reduction in self-commitment starts. Figure 3–2 shows the percentage of capacity from 

starts by commitment process. For all generation participation offers in the day-ahead market 

by commitment status, see Figure 3–9.

As shown above, 57% of started capacity in 2024 was a result of the day-ahead market, which 

continues to be the primary commitment process. The day-ahead market is the preferred 

method of start-up for resources with longer lead times. However, a limiting factor on the 

number of day-ahead commitments is that the optimization algorithm is restricted to a 48-hour 
window.30 Large base-load resources with long lead times and long run times may not appear 

economic to the day-ahead market commitment algorithm. Some market participants choose to 

self-commit these resources at their economic minimum to have them online and available for 

incremental dispatch.

Within the operating day, commitment flexibility is limited by resource start-up times. As the 

operating hour approaches, fewer resources are eligible to be started. The reliability unit 
commitment processes—day-ahead,31 intra-day, short-term, and manual—represent about 18% 

of the started capacity. Day-ahead RUC starts are concentrated in the intervals starting at 3 AM, 

10AM, 2 PM and 8 PM. Intra-day RUC starts occur mostly in the midday hours between 11 am 

and 3 pm, covering peak hours. The short-term RUC starts increase until late evening. On top of 

resource and forecast uncertainty, RUC commitments also fill any need for additional ramp-able 

capacity. The ramp product, implemented in March 2022, and the uncertainty product, 
implemented in July 2023, were expected to help reduce these amounts.32 

Figure 3–3 shows start-up instructions issued by fuel type. For coal and gas resources, most 

start-ups occur in the day-ahead market. Gas resources utilizing combined-cycle technology 

have the highest portion of starts in the day-ahead, at over 94%. This result is expected given 

the lower variable costs and more flexible operating parameters for these resources relative to 

other gas units as well as resources utilizing simple cycle technology and/or higher cost fuel, 

such as coal. 

Figure 3–3  Origin of start-up instructions by fuel type 

For gas-fired generators with simple-cycle combustion turbine technology (internal combustion 

and steam turbines), the day-ahead market accounted for 68% of their total starts. This is a 

decline from 2021 and 2022 which accounted for three-quarters of their total starts, but nearly 

flat from 2023. Steam turbine starts increased from 65% to 71% in the day-ahead market. The 

31 This is day-ahead reliability unit commitment process, not the day-ahead market.  
32 The ramp product is discussed in further detail in Section 3.2.7.1, and the uncertainty product is 
discussed in Section 3.2.7.2.

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

percentage of starts in the intra-day RUC, the second most common type of start, decreased for 

combined-cycle and simple-cycle gas resources. 

The SPP market allows participants to self-commit resources rather than have the market choose 

which units to run. While there may be good reasons for this, the practice can distort prices, 

offer and bid behavior, market outcomes, and investment signals. This is particularly true if a 

large proportion of the region’s capacity frequently self-commits.  

The volume of dispatched megawatts from self-committed resources remains a significant 
portion of the total dispatch megawatt volumes.33 For 2024, 30% of the energy produced was 

from self-committed resources and hence was not economically selected by the day-ahead 
market’s centralized unit commitment process.34 Self-commitment shifts the merit order of the 

supply curve by treating the self-committed generators as price insensitive at their minimum, 

which shifts the supply curve to the right. The expected result of a rightward shift in supply is a 

decline in the marginal price of energy. 

Figure 3–4 shows dispatch megawatts by fuel type by commitment type for each year of the 
study period.35 A large portion of the self-committed resources have longer lead times 

compared to market-committed resources. In fact, a large portion of self-committed resources 

have a lead time greater than 24 hours. 

33 All output from a self-committed unit is counted as self. For more detail on this and other metrics, see 
the MMU’s whitepaper on self-commitment, Self-committing in SPP markets: Overview, impacts, and 
recommendations. 
34 Reliability unit commitments that continued to run in the day-ahead market were considered a market 
commitment. 
35 We only include Market and Self statuses as less than 1% come from resources using other commitment 
statuses.

Figure 3–4  Dispatch megawatt hours by fuel type by commitment type 

In contrast to long lead resources, fast-start resources are those that are dispatchable, can start, 

synchronize, begin injecting energy within 10 minutes of SPP notification, and have a minimum 
run time of one hour or less.36 Market storage resources with a discharge time of 60 minutes or 

less also meet the fast-start criteria. In 2016, FERC issued a Notice of Proposed Rulemaking 

(NOPR) on fast-start pricing processes as a part of a broad initiative aimed at improving price 
formation in regulated wholesale power markets in the United States.37 SPP’s final proposal for 

its fast-start pricing practices were accepted by FERC in June 2019 with an implementation date 
of May 2022.38 

Fast-start resources can sit off-line near real-time and respond to ramp and uncertainty needs – 

but only if they are not committed in the day-ahead market. Historically, SPP has seen a large 

percentage of the fast-start resources clearing in the day-ahead market. Unfortunately, the 

proportion of megawatts cleared in day-head from these resources has only continued to 

increase. Figure 3–5 shows the megawatts cleared by fast start resources in the day-ahead 

market compared to megawatts dispatched incremental to day-ahead in real-time.

quick-start resources, regardless of minimum run time.

RM17-3-000.

Figure 3–5  Day-ahead cleared megawatts vs incremental real-time 

The chart shows that resources meeting the fast-start criteria post-implementation appear to 

have cleared a higher percentage in the day-ahead market than in real-time after the new fast-

start logic implementation. In 2022, 87% of megawatts coming from fast-start resources came 

from the day-ahead. This has since increased to 96% in 2023 and 95% in 2024. Of the fast-start 

megawatts cleared in the day-ahead market, 87% came from natural gas resources while the 

remaining 13% came from hydroelectric plants. Fuel oil and other fuel fast-start resources had 

most of their dispatched megawatts committed in real-time (84% and 100%, respectively). 

However, these resources accounted for roughly 1% of the total dispatch megawatts coming 

from fast-start resources across both day-ahead and real-time.  

Another key objective of the fast-start logic was to reduce uplift in the market, particularly for 

fast-start resources. The expectation was that higher energy prices set by the fast-start 

resources’ composite offers would reduce the need for uplift. Figure 3–6 and Figure 3–7 show 

the 2024 day-ahead and real-time make-whole payments, by month, for the 86 resources 

qualifying as fast-starts post implementation.

Total make-whole payments to fast-start resources were roughly $17.8 million, down from $19.7

  GENERATION SCHEDULING 

The day-ahead market provides market participants with the ability to submit offers to sell 

energy, regulation-up service, regulation-down service, spinning reserve, and supplemental 

reserve, and/or to submit bids to purchase energy. The day-ahead market co-optimizes the 

State of the Market 2024 

68 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

clearing of energy and operating reserve products out of the offered capacity. All day-ahead 

market products are traded and settled on an hourly basis. 

The Integrated Marketplace has a limited day-ahead must-offer provision that was intended to 

incentivize load-serving entities with generation assets to participate in the day-ahead market. 

Market participants that are non-compliant are assessed a penalty based on the amount of 

capacity available in the day-ahead market relative to the market participant’s peak hourly real-
time load.39 In 2024, 4 asset owners were assessed penalties totaling $79,205 in must-offer 

penalties. The must-offer violations occurred over 2 distinct days. The total penalty is a 

significant increase from 2023’s total of $49,745. 

While this provision does highly encourage available generation to be offered, it does not 

impose a penalty for excessive outages, which has been cited as a reason for conservative 

operations in the past. The day-ahead must-offer provision also does not tie into Attachment 

AA, which defines the resource adequacy requirement in the SPP tariff. The MMU continues to 

recommend updating the day-ahead must offer requirement and addressing FERC’s concerns. 

See further discussion in Section 7.2. 

In 2024, participation in the day-ahead market dropped among load-serving entities that also 

own generation assets. The largest decrease came from load-serving entity-owned fossil fuel 

resources, driven primarily by an uptick in capacity on outage. Participation by merchant 

generation was significantly higher for fossil fuel resources, but considerably lower for variable 

energy resources compared to load-serving entities, consistent with previous years. Merchant 

generators also saw a decrease in outages among their fossil fuel resources, but an uptick in 

outages among variable energy resources. Figure 3–8 also shows that merchant generators self-

commit resources at a much lower rate than load-serving entities. This is likely because 

merchant generators have incentive structures in place based primarily on market outcomes. 

The MMU is continuing to investigate the cause of the larger outage percentage for load-

serving entities.  

39The requirement is limited in the sense that not all resources or capacity must be offered. Only market 
participants with generation assets that serve load are subject to the must-offer requirement, and they are 
required to offer enough generation to cover most of their load plus reserve obligations, per asset owner. 
An alternative way to satisfy the provision is to offer all resource capacity not on outage.

  PHYSICAL PARAMETERS 

The purpose of the centralized unit commitment processes is to commit sufficient resources to 

serve load, subject to transmission and resource constraints, while minimizing cost. The 

centralized unit commitment process can minimize commitment costs because it has 

information such as the amount of capacity available, the current transmission topology, and the 

State of the Market 2024 

70 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

operational parameters of each resource. Regarding the latter, SPP allows participants to self-

report the physical operating parameters of each resource. Per the market protocols, these 

physical operating parameters should reflect the underlying physical capabilities of a resource.  

A resource’s physical parameters determine how often and how quickly a resource can be 

started, how long a resource must run once online, and how quickly a resource can change its 

output (ramp), among other things. Resources with physical parameters that limit how quickly it 

can change its output and/or its availability for commitment or dispatch are, in general, less 

flexible. Moreover, a resource’s physical parameters can indicate what services, outside of 

energy, a resource can provide the market. This includes regulation, ramp, uncertainty, 

contingency, and quick-start capability. The growth in variable energy resources has increased 

the demand for ancillary services, such as regulation, ramp, and uncertainty, and requires a 

system with more flexibility to respond to sudden changes in output. Thus, to be effective and 

efficient, an accurate representation of the physical capabilities for resources is very important 

Figure 3–10 provides a summary of the average values for a subset of physical parameters 

across the fleet, both weighted by capacity and a simple average across all resources. 

Additionally, as resources with a common fuel frequently have relatively similar physical 

capabilities, the table also shows these values by fuel type.

The two nuclear units are the least flexible with almost no dispatchable range (economic 

maximum minus economic minimum). While they report relatively short minimum run and down 

times and start up times, these resources are exclusively self-committed, making run and startup 

times less relevant. Coal units, which are generally older steam turbines, making up roughly 21% 

of the region’s installed capacity, have long startup times, slow ramp rates, and long minimum 

State of the Market 2024 

71 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

run times. Natural gas resources, which consist of a combination of steam and combustion 

turbines, have much shorter startup times, faster ramp rates, and an average minimum run time 

of just shy of one day. These resources are much more flexible and account for roughly 36% of 

the region’s installed capacity. Looked at through this lens, the most flexible resources are solar, 

water, and wind resources and, to a slightly lesser degree, oil resources. However, though they 

provide the most flexibility via shorter startup times and faster ramp rates, most are variable 

energy resources which represent the highest uncertainty in available capacity and are the least 

controllable by SPP. Together, these resources make up roughly 40% of the region’s installed 

capacity.  

In an efficient market, physical parameters should reflect the physical capabilities of a resource. 

However, because physical parameters are self-reported, opportunities exist to set physical 

parameters in such a way as to significantly influence or hedge against certain outcomes related 

to commitment and dispatch. Examples of this behavior that the MMU has observed include: 

•  Changing real-time economic minimum operating limits in the real-time balancing 

market to match day-ahead commitment and clearing. This allows resources to avoid 

being dispatched below their day-ahead clearing in real-time to serve a contract or 

hedge against buying back their day-ahead position at a loss while ramp-constrained.  

•  Depressing ramp-down rates to avoid becoming ramp constrained and missing price 

spikes. This allows inframarginal resources to avoid being ramped down during a 

transient downward price excursion, becoming ramp constrained and missing out on 

producer surplus or potentially buying back a lucrative day-ahead position at a loss, 

when prices rebound. Limiting ramp rates creates unnecessary inflexibility in the fleet of 

resources managed by SPP, and leads to an inefficient, more costly dispatch solution.  

•  Extending minimum runtimes beyond the day-ahead economic evaluation window (two 

days). The day-ahead market solution minimizes forecasted production costs, including 

uplift, across a two-day window to help normalize production costs across resource 

types. Resources with more expensive starts may be the best economic choice when 

evaluated over the two-day period. However, if the resource with a minimum runtime 

longer than two days is no longer economic after the evaluation period, it is treated as a 

“must run” for the remainder of its minimum runtime and is eligible to be made whole to 

its mitigated costs. The minimum runtime parameter can be used to ensure a resource 

meets contractual obligations or to burn its fuel as a hedge against a take or pay 

contract. 

State of the Market 2024 

72 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

Using physical parameters to effectively override what would otherwise be the outcome of the 

central commitment and dispatch process leads to sub-optimal, inefficient market outcomes. 

Moreover, this behavior can be a means to exert market power by changing the quantity and 

potentially the clearing price of energy, but not to a degree that would fail SPP’s conduct and 

impact test. As such, the MMU maintains that physical parameters should reflect the current 

underlying physical capabilities of a resource and that this should be included in the tariff, not 

just the market protocols.  

3.2  DISPATCH 

  RESOURCE AVAILABILITY 

Resource availability represents the generating capacity available to the market to be committed 

and subsequently dispatched to serve load and ancillary service requirements. Available capacity 

has been a growing concern for SPP. While the region used to have a large excess of generating 

capacity, the margin between available capacity and load has been shrinking as conventional 

resources retire and load grows. Additionally, a greater proportion of SPP’s resources are 

variable energy resources that are not fully controllable by SPP. Key to maintaining reliability is 

an understanding of what resources are available to the market (economically), to the region for 

reliability (through emergency maximum capabilities and capacity operating in a “Reliability 

Status”) and knowing the main drivers of resource unavailability.  

In 2024, average hourly capacity available to the market in real-time (represented by economic 

maximums) dropped from 58.3 GW in 2022 to 55.6 GW in 2024. Section 3.1.2 discusses how, 

relative to previous years, 2024 saw a decrease in economic availability needed to meet load and 

reserve requirements. In line with Figure 3-8 and Figure 3-9, outages are key to the decreased 

availability of capacity.  

Looking at generation availability relative to demand shows the margin available to the region 

via economic dispatch. Figure 3–11 shows, for each day, the minimum hourly capacity available 

from all internal resources registered in the market as a percentage of load plus contingency 

reserve for that same hour for 2022, 2023, and 2024.  

State of the Market 2024 

73 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

Figure 3–11  Minimum capacity available as a percentage of load by day 

The maximum amount of capacity offered during any hour in the real-time market was just over 

74 GW while the maximum demand was just under 55 GW. Including capacity available via 

dispatching resources up to their emergency maximum or from resources using a reliability 

commitment status, the highest capacity available was 77.4 GW. While these numbers indicate 

SPP has sufficient capacity to cover internal demand in theory, the actual margin between 

available capacity and demand varies significantly. During 2024, minimum availability as a 

percentage of load during a given day averaged 141%, a six-percentage point decrease from 

2023, and an 11-percentage point decrease from 2022. The minimum average availability as a 

percentage of load was 96%, occurring on January 14. This was the most critical day of the 

winter weather storm experienced by the region in early 2024. The next two lowest average daily 

availability as a percentage of load (both 98%) were August 26 and 27, the former during which 

the region declared an EEA1 during midday. Including capacity available via emergency 

maximums and capacity in a reliability commitment status only increases the lowest availability 

compared to load days to 100% and 102%, respectively.  

Figure 3–12 breaks out capacity by status. Capacity with an economic status is any capacity 

offered in up to a resource’s economic maximum. Similarly, capacity with an emergency 

maximum or reliability status is any capacity above and up to a resource’s emergency maximum 

or offered in under a “Reliability” commitment status. This capacity is only available if SPP 

determines there is a reliability event and often either represents megawatts that may be riskier 

to dispatch up to or resources that, if called upon, risk not being available later. Thus, this 

capacity truly is less available than that offered in up to a resource’s economic maximum. Finally, 

capacity with either a derate or outage status is unavailable due to a reported derate or outage.

Together, capacity in one of these categories makes up 75-80% of installed capacity. The 
remaining capacity was simply not offered into the market.40 

As Figure 3–12 demonstrates, economic availability peaked during the winter and summer. This 

is in line with when the region experiences its highest loads. Average monthly economic 

availability ranged from 50-60% of estimated installed capacity and is lowest the same months 

outages are the highest, which are the region’s shoulder months. These are April and May in the 

spring and October and November in the fall. The average monthly capacity available via 

resources’ emergency maximums or from resources in a “Reliability” status remained constant at 

around 3% of estimated installed capacity. Average monthly capacity on either derate or full 

outage ranged from 11% to nearly 30% of estimated installed capacity. 

These results indicate that, on average, SPP has access to just over half of the estimated installed 

capacity through traditional economic dispatch. Another significant portion of capacity is 

derated or on outage. Whether capacity on derate or outage poses a reliability risk depends on 

whether the outage was scheduled or forced, which is discussed further in the Resource Outages 

section. Whether this capacity poses a potential risk to reliability depends on many factors we 

do not discuss in this chapter, notably the assumptions of the Loss of Load Expectation (LOLE) 

study.  

Figure 3–12  Capacity status by type 

As the proportion of variable energy resources (VERs) in SPP’s supply mix continues to grow, the 

system’s need to count on the availability of conventional resources increases. In 2024,

conventional resources accounted for 80% of the available generation on average, ranging from

Figure 3–13 compares internal conventional capacity to its actual availability and load plus

controllable by SPP to prevent operating reserve or energy shortages without adequate 

improvements to transmission and investment in energy storage or complementary generation 

represents a real risk to system reliability.

resources and imports.

in 178 hours or 2% of the year. For 522 hours, or 6% of the year, the region experienced 

conventional availability that was within 5% of the load obligation or less. While most of these 

hours occurred during the summer season when demand tends to be highest, a significant 

number happened during the shoulder months of April, May, October, and November.

the winter storm in January, specifically between January 14 and January 16. This demonstrates

imports throughout the year, not just during the peak periods represented by the winter and

Metrics that reflect daily, monthly, or seasonal averages do not reflect the extremes of the 

period. Reliability within loss of load expectations requires planning for those extremes. Figure

80%. In two months, this figure exceeded 100%, at 103% in January and 101% in August. In both 

cases, the system was in an advisory or emergency status. However, any time load is at least 90% 

of available generation is a period where excess capacity is limited, and system reliability is at 

greater risk.

SPP enforces a resource adequacy requirement (RAR) during the summer and, going forward, 
winter seasons as part of the broader supply adequacy process to ensure reliability.41 The RAR 

requires all load responsible entities to accredit enough capacity to cover their net peak load 

plus the planning reserve margin (PRM).  

Figure 3–16 shows the average daily real-time availability of conventional accredited capacity 

that is registered internally to SPP (and hence, whose performance is directly measurable) 

throughout winter and summer 2024. As demonstrated by Figure 3–16, availability of accredited 

capacity differs significantly between the winter and summer seasons. On average, 88% of 

accredited capacity was offered economically. Accounting for capacity offered via resources’ 

emergency maxes, roughly 92% of accredited capacity was available on average. There was 

significant variability in the total availability across the season. While wind was the most variable, 

the large swings in capacity stemmed primarily from a large drop in availability among coal and 

natural gas resources during the winter storm due to outages and changes in hydro availability 

throughout the season.

Figure 3–16  Availability of accredited capacity 

Availability in the summer was less variable but was also lower on average, with only 84% of 

capacity offered in economically and 87% when including capacity up to resources’ emergency 

maxes. Every fuel type excluding water and natural gas saw a measurable drop in average 

availability between winter and summer. Outages in the nuclear fleet later in the summer and 

large decreases in hydro resource availability are primarily responsible for the overall downward 

trend in availability as the summer progressed. Another difference in the summer season was 

that, on average, there was a greater spread between what resources offered in economically 

(via economic maximum) and for reliability purposes (emergency maximum). As the capacity 

above a resource’s economic maximum can come at higher risk, a higher proportion of 

accredited capacity coming from this range may indicate greater risk that the region runs short 

of accredited capacity in during high load periods. 

For the resource adequacy requirement to be effective in supporting reliability, the MMU 

recommends that the accreditation process allocate the cost of reliability based on resource 

performance. SPP and stakeholders recognized that the current accreditation methodologies did 

not render accurate representations of resource performance and filed a performance-based 

accreditation for conventional resources and an effective load carrying capability (ELCC) process 
for variable energy resources with FERC in early 2024.42  

A resource adequacy requirement is not sufficient to guarantee reliability. Currently, the tariff 

lacks appropriate incentives for resources to remain available during the resource adequacy 

42 Southwest Power Pool, Inc., Submission of Tariff Revisions to Implement Effective Load Carrying 
Capability Methodology and Performance Based Accreditation, Docket No. ER24-1317-000 (February 23, 
2024) 

State of the Market 2024 

79 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

season as there is no mechanism that addresses units that were claimed as capacity but become 

unavailable after an LRE has submitted its workbook. Load responsible entities who have 

capacity that is accredited but is unavailable for part or all the season will not be subject to the 

deficiency payment, the main tariff mechanism incentivizing load responsible entities to carry an 

appropriate level of accredited capacity. If SPP adopts the proposed Performance Based 

Accreditation (PBA), forced outages not defined as Out of Management Control by NERC will 

factor into a conventional resource’s accreditation, addressing part of this issue. However, the 

MMU has raised concerns that averaging performance over 21 months will dilute the impact of 

outages and correspondingly, substantially weaken the incentive to be available.  

Additionally, a small but significant amount of accredited capacity comes from a range typically 

above resources’ economic maximum; another portion comes from resources using a Reliability 

commitment status. The MMU remains concerned over the practice of accrediting resources up 

to a capacity amount that is generally only reflected in a resource’s emergency maximum and 

not defining a limited set of circumstances that accredited capacity may be offered in a 

Reliability commitment status. This creates a potential reliability risk as while this capacity is 

technically available for reliability, its true availability and risk is less known. In some cases, 

increasing a unit’s output level to its emergency maximum can put it at an increased risk of 

tripping offline, leaving the region without access to the entire capacity of that accredited 

resource. Capacity coming from resources in a Reliability commitment status is not generally 

online and the risk of a failure to start could limit the value of this capacity.  

Without a mechanism to incentivize or ensure availability, there is a disconnect between the 

capacity SPP accredits for resource adequacy and the actual capacity available to operate the 

system reliably. SPP and its stakeholders should continue to work on new mechanisms and 

evaluate the efficacy of current incentives that reward resources that perform more reliably and 

are available when needed by SPP operations. The MMU also continues to recommend that a 

resource adequacy requirement be applied to all four seasons. While forecasted demand tends 

to be lower in the shoulder seasons, the region continues to see more intervals where SPP must 

declare a Resource Advisory or above during these times.  

Overall, the MMU has the same four critical recommendations related to supply adequacy as we 

published in the 2023 State of the \Market report, with slight modifications based on lessons 

learned over the past three years: 

1)  If SPP is to rely on any resource to provide energy, then that resource should be 

available. The resource adequacy process should consider all outages to represent 

expected availability more accurately. It should also include an analysis of outage 

correlation between resources in similar areas using the same fuels and/or technologies 

State of the Market 2024 

80 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

and across all seasons. Deliverability should also be considered, like in the effective load 

carrying capability methodology.  

2)  There should be meaningful incentives related to reliability. There should be mechanisms 

to incentivize reliability attributes and these mechanisms should be market-based to the 

greatest extent possible. These reliability attributes include but are not limited to 

flexibility on outage timing and duration, dual-fuel capability, and enhanced 

winterization. 

3)  A more frequent resource adequacy requirement, such as a seasonal requirement, should 

be developed. The February 2021 winter weather event and December 2022 winter storm 

both demonstrated the importance of having an accurate expectation of resource 

availability during cold weather, not just summer peak. Our data also demonstrate low 

availability levels during the shoulder seasons when most planned outages are taken. 

The MMU recommends exploring the feasibility of a four-season resource adequacy 

requirement.  

4)  SPP should plan for shocks to generator availability including adverse weather events, 

pipeline outages, wind turbine icing, and solar eclipses. Express attention should be paid 

to the pattern and correlation of outages between resource types and locations. SPP 

should implement mitigation measures to ensure the reliable operation of the grid under 

all circumstances. This may include strategic investments in transmission to enhance 

deliverability of capacity, development of energy storage resources, and price sensitive 

demand. 

  RESOURCE OUTAGES 

Resource outages are the main contributor to resource unavailability for both accredited and 

unaccredited capacity. Two major reasons for resource outages are generator maintenance and 

a forced event, such as an equipment failure. Maintenance outages are those submitted to SPP 

for study and approval ahead of time. As the reliability coordinator and balancing authority, SPP 

determines a maintenance margin, which is the amount of capacity allocated for planned and 

opportunity generator outages at a given time. SPP approves, denies, or reschedules these 

outages to ensure system reliability using the maintenance margin and following the outage 
coordination methodology.43 

Forced outages, by contrast, are generally unpredictable, resulting from sudden equipment 

failure, fuel supply disruptions, or extreme events such as icing or flooding. However, the MMU 

43 SPP Reliability Coordinator Outage Coordination Methodology. 

State of the Market 2024 

81 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

has also seen instances where market participants put a resource on forced outage for economic 

reasons, such as not wanting to staff the resource or to take an outage that was not approved 

by SPP. Forced outages are not known or studied in advance by SPP operations and may pose a 

reliability risk, especially during periods of capacity scarcity. As such, minimizing the frequency 

and duration of forced outages is a critical piece of ensuring the reliable operation of the grid. 

This requires creating the right incentives for resources to mitigate exposure to potential shocks 

such as freezing and penalizing resources that use forced outages inappropriately. 

Overall, from 2023 to 2024, there was an increase in total outaged capacity of over 8%. More 

specifically, forced and maintenance outages increased by over 14% and 1% respectively. This 

can be seen in Figure 3–17, which shows capacity derated and taken out-of-service by reason—

forced or maintenance. Each reason is further categorized by fuel type.  

Figure 3–17  Resource outages and derates 

In 2024, capacity forced out-of-service accounted for the largest portion of outaged capacity. 

This is followed by capacity out-of-service for maintenance, forced derates, and finally, by 

maintenance derates respectively. Coal provided 25% of total generation and accounted for 

about 26% of outaged capacity in 2024. Natural gas represented the greatest ratio of outaged 

capacity – providing 29% of total generation and about 55% of outaged capacity. Inversely, wind 

provided nearly 38% of total generation and only about 10% of outaged capacity. 

The primary reasons listed for maintenance outage include routine generator maintenance 

(44%) and other equipment failure (13%). Testing and inspections accounted for another 14%. 

The primary reasons for forced outages included other equipment failure (45%), unknown (12%), 

and fuel supply (9%). Weather, other, and fuel system accounted for another 17%.

rate – 16% since 2023. This is a concerning trend as forced outages potentially pose a greater 

reliability risk. While long-term outages have continued to increase, these outages are studied 

by SPP to ensure they do not pose an undue risk to reliability.

winter weather event in January and in the later summer and early fall months. Overall, short-

short-term outages have decreased by around 17% - partly driven by a lack of extreme events

outages, the MMU remains concerned about the use of short-term forced outages, especially of 

accredited capacity.  

Figure 3–19 shows outaged capacity for short-term outages and derates of seven days or less.  

Figure 3–19  Short-term outages, seven days or less 

Correlated output, including correlated outages, is a growing area of concern in SPP. In 2018, 

SPP and stakeholders recognized that maintaining reliability as the region sees a large increase 

in variable energy resources, whose output tends to be highly correlated, requires a new 

approach to accreditation for these resources. Following the industry trend, SPP filed a tariff 

revision with FERC in February 2024 that would adopt an effective load carrying capability 

methodology for accreditation of variable energy resources to explicitly account for the 

reliability risk associated with their output that is both highly correlated and not fully 

controllable. However, variable energy resources are not the only resources that pose reliability 

risks due to correlated output. In 2024, SPP also filed a winter season only, Fuel Assurance 

methodology with FERC that would act as an addition to the proposed accreditation process for 

conventional resources. The Fuel Assurance policy will further adjust a resource’s performance if 

their outage was 1) fuel-related and 2) was considered incremental to the amount of forced 

outages assumed by the LOLE study.  

Finally, SPP and stakeholders filed revisions establishing a multi-day economic commitment 

process that are intended to signal resources to procure gas ahead of time and at the most 

State of the Market 2024 

84 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

economic rate possible while protecting them from some fuel uncertainty risk.44 SPP and 

stakeholders also filed Tariff revisions that modifies the proposed conventional resource 

accreditation practices to limit the accredited megawatts for resources that have been on full or 
partial forced outages due to fuel constraints during hours in which they were most needed.45 In 

theory, this approach would account for correlated output in conventional resource 

accreditation, creating incentives for generators to invest in infrastructure or contracts which 

would either firm their respective natural gas supplies or allow the units to operate with a 

secondary fuel source located on-site.  

Figure 3–20 and Figure 3–21 show the distribution of the percentage of capacity on forced 

outage by fuel type during the summer season and proposed winter season, respectively. While 

percentage of fleet is a good measure of correlation between outages, the reliability impact of 

these correlated outages also depends on how many resources are on outage and the amount 

of capacity on outage. If a small percentage of coal resources is on outage but this represents a 

large amount of capacity, this could present a greater reliability issue than a larger percentage 

of oil units on outage that represents a small amount of total capacity. Thus, the call outs 

identify the median amount of capacity and number of units on forced outage by fuel type.

The region experiences outage correlation among resources of the same fuel type to a 

measurable degree in both winter and summer. While summer tends to see a greater median 

amount of capacity on outage at the same time, the expected amount or distribution of outages 

occurring at the same time among resources of the same type varies to a greater degree. This 

indicates that while summer may see more correlated outages, winter may experience greater 

unpredictability.  

Wind resources typically have the lowest percent of total capacity on forced outage at the same 

time (5% in the winter, 4% in the summer), whereas coal resources have the highest (9% in the 

winter, 13% in the summer). Fuel oil and coal resources have a significant difference in the 

median amount of capacity on outage at the same time between winter and summer. For these 

resources, the median amount of capacity on outage at any given time is significantly higher in 

summer. In terms of megawatts of capacity, the amount of fuel oil capacity on concurrent 

outage is 30% higher or 44 megawatts. For coal, these figures are 50% or 1,000 megawatts. 

Natural gas and wind resources tend to have the largest number of resources on forced outage 

at the same time. For natural gas, this represents a median of 2,300 to 2,800 megawatts on 

outage. Wind forced outages generally account for 1,400 to 1,700 megawatts of capacity.  

The differences in capacity on outage versus total units demonstrates how wind resources tend 

to have smaller installed capacities, natural gas resources are slightly larger, and coal resources 

are, on average, the largest resources. Each pose different challenges for reliability and reserves. 

Having a smaller number of resources on simultaneous forced outage versus a larger number 

with the same capacity does not matter from a capacity and reserves standpoint. In general, it is 

more common and likely that two larger coal resources go on forced outage at the same time 

State of the Market 2024 

86 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

than many natural gas resources totaling the same capacity. However, from a reliability 

standpoint, having a larger number of resources go on forced outage simultaneously can pose a 

greater challenge. This is due to the impact of multiple resources’ output (or lack thereof) on 

congestion. The more flowgates impacted by forced outages, the more SPP must manage for 

congestion.  

Correlated forced outages, whether posing a challenge for reserves, managing congestion, 

reliability, or a combination of the three, should be central to the supply adequacy process. 

While the proposed ELCC methodology for accreditation explicitly considers correlated output, 

the proposed PBA methodology for accreditation for conventional resources does not. The data 

above suggests that correlated outages among conventional resources do have a significant 

impact to availability and hence, should be considered in the accreditation process.  

In addition to identifying correlated outages, having the ability to understand the causes allows 

for a better prediction of forced outages and to take steps to mitigate their causes. Currently, 

outage data are often incomplete or insufficient to determine driving causes behind forced 

outages. More accurate, complete, and standardized outage data would allow SPP and the 

MMU to identify common causes of forced outages. 

A frequent cause of forced outages, and often correlated forced outages, is fuel procurement. 

This can include supply chain issues, deliverability issues, quality issues, and financial/credit risk 

issues, particularly related to the misalignment of the gas and electric markets. Although SPP 

and market participants adjusted the day-ahead market commitment schedule through the 

stakeholder process, timely natural gas nominations are still due prior to market participants 
receiving their commitment schedule.46 Depending on the pipeline’s capacity and the type of 

service the gas generator has, this could have multiple undesired consequences including 

market participants not procuring fuel without a day-ahead commitment then not being able to 

procure gas economically (or at all) in real-time or procuring natural gas without a day-head 

commitment then being charged parking or other fees when the gas is not used.  

Several wholesale natural gas and electricity organizations, including SPP, have been exploring 

new market rules and policies that are intended to incentivize procurement of the most reliable 

lowest cost natural gas service, firm timely natural gas service, when appropriate, as well as 

investment in secondary and/or stored fuel sources for capacity resources. In response to the 
February 2021 winter weather event, the North American Energy Standards Board, or NAESB47, 

46 Docket No. ER19-2681. 
47 NAESB serves as an industry forum for the development and promotion of standards which help develop 
a seamless marketplace for wholesale and retail natural gas and electricity, as recognized by its customers, 
business community, participants, and regulatory entities. 

State of the Market 2024 

87 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

initiated a second Gas-Electric Harmonization (GEH) forum that began in August 2022 and 

culminated in a final recommendation report submitted to the FERC chair and NERC president 
on July 28, 2023.48 Due to the discord across the gas and electricity sectors, the GEH final report 

suggested federal legislative action would be needed to create a reliability-based regulatory 

body for the natural gas industry to get natural gas stakeholders to implement the Report’s 

recommendations. In October 2023, a coalition of North American market monitors, led by the 

SPP MMU, submitted joint comments to FERC and NERC in general support of the GEH’s efforts 
and recommendations.49 

  OUTAGE COORDINATION 

To balance the need to perform routine and preventative maintenance and to maintain grid 

reliability, SPP uses a centralized outage coordination process. This process includes setting a 

maintenance margin, which is the amount of capacity that can safely be on planned or 

maintenance outage based on expected grid conditions at a given time. Numerous factors, 

including an aging fleet, generator retirements, and an increase in variable energy resource 

penetration have led to a narrowing maintenance margin over time. This means to ensure 

outages are efficiently prioritized and approved, the accuracy and transparency of the outage 

scheduling process is ever more important.  

Contributing to the efficiency of the outage coordination process is outage submittal data. The 

outage coordination methodology requires a reason and planned end date for the outage at 
both the time of the outage submittal and at the submittal of each change.50 SPP uses these 

data to study each outage to determine the priority of the outage, if the outage as submitted 

will pose any reliability risks, and to ensure the total amount of capacity on outage does not 

exceed the maintenance margin during the proposed timeframe. Because outage submissions

subsequent kick-off meeting for the wholesale gas quadrant, gas producers unanimously urged dismissal

Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

are intended to be used for assessing real-time and future reliability of the bulk electric system, 
it is critical that the outage information submitted to SPP is accurate.51  

The MMU has observed insufficient, omitted, delayed, and incorrect outage information. The 

MMU maintains material misstatements of outage information could be considered providing 
false information to the RTO and may result in referral to FERC.52 However, these data are hard if 

not impossible to validate after the fact. Instead, greater care should be taken at the time of 

submission to ensure that outages are not studied or approved until all necessary data are 

submitted and validated for accuracy.  

Another area of concern is outage extensions. An outage’s original scope of work cannot always 

be completed in the original scheduled timeframe. Therefore, the current outage coordination 

methodology allows an outage to be extended. However, outage extensions can reduce 

efficiency of the outage coordination process. Regardless of the outage extension priority, 

outage extensions are given priority for approval, similar to forced outages. Therefore, outage 

extensions have the potential to be inappropriately prioritized higher than new outages, 

resulting in denial of the new outages. In addition, outage extensions decrease the amount of 

available maintenance margin and, due to their unexpected nature, can adversely affect other 

processes such as the congestion hedging market.  

Figure 3–22 shows generator outage extensions that are greater than one week by duration. 

Figure 3–22  Generator outage extension count by duration, greater than one week 

51 SPP Reliability Coordinator Outage Coordination Methodology, Section 1 
52 18 CFR § 35.41 

State of the Market 2024 

89 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

In 2024, there were 929 outages extended longer than one week, a 14% increase from 2023. The 

MMU continues to encourage SPP to implement processes and mechanisms to properly 

schedule, classify, report, and prioritize outage extensions.  

The MMU remains concerned about the improper use of outage extensions, especially outage 

extensions of long duration. The MMU has observed outages extended for reasons different 

than the original outage reason, resulting in misclassified and nontransparent outage 

extensions. Misclassified outages can affect out-of-market budgetary items, such as state 

recovery rates and taxes. For some performance-based resource adequacy calculations, 

misclassified outages could affect the amount of capacity accredited under SPP’s stakeholder 
approved performance-based accreditation approach.53  

Even though there are legitimate reasons for resources to be on outage, proper incentives and 

procedures can promote efficiency, reliability, and proper outage behavior. To these ends, the 

MMU recommends SPP: 

•  Bolster the outage coordination methodology by requiring all necessary supporting 

information be submitted before an outage can be submitted for review and approval. In 

addition, SPP should set in place a process for verifying and validating the submitted 

data to ensure the proper outage priority and status are assigned.  

•  Validate that an outage extension is needed to finish the original outage’s scope of work. 

When this cannot be validated, SPP should treat the extension as a new outage request. 

•  Develop a clear link between the outage coordination and resource adequacy processes 

to ensure transparency and accountability. This will not only improve the accuracy of the 

accreditation process but will provide a proper incentive for resources to not misclassify 

or misuse outages.  

  DEMAND FOR RELIABILITY 

While SPP attempts to commit as much generation as possible in the day-ahead market, Figure 

3–2 noted that about 14% of SPP start-up instructions by capacity originated from SPP reliability 

unit commitment processes. These commitments (and decommitments) result from changes in 

forecasted and actual load between the day-ahead market and real-time. To understand the 

53 Such as the Generator Testing Task Force’s recommendation of EFOF’. See "GTTF Performance 
Accreditation Educational Overview_Final.pdf”, available at 
https://www.spp.org/Documents/66148/SAWG%20Agenda%20and%20Background%20Materials%202021
1208.zip. 

State of the Market 2024 

90 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

need for reliability commitments, it is useful to discuss the different assumptions, requirements, 

and rules that are used in the reliability unit commitment processes after the day-ahead market. 

A fundamental difference between the day-ahead and reliability unit commitment studies is the 

definition of demand. In the day-ahead market, demand is determined by bids submitted by the 

market participants whereas, in the real-time market, demand is physical. Demand bids in the 

day-ahead market average around 96%-99% of the real-time values, as shown in Figure 2–6. 

Other smaller differences between the two markets include losses and operating reserves. 

These differences are referred to as resource gaps (i.e., a gap in meeting demand) between the 

day-ahead and real-time markets. The resource gap is the excess price-following, physical 

generation cleared in the day-ahead market that was not needed in real-time. A negative 

resource gap would indicate that the total generation cleared in the day-ahead market is 

insufficient to serve real-time demand. The resource gap is typically positive, indicating more 

dispatchable generation is cleared in the day-ahead market than was necessary to serve real-

time load. 

The primary drivers for the resource gaps are:  

1)  differences in virtual supply net of virtual demand,  

2)  differences in real-time wind generation compared to wind cleared in the day-ahead 

market, and 

3)  real-time net exports exceeding day-ahead net exports.  

It is generally true that the day-ahead market clears less wind generation than is produced in 

real-time. The mismatch is partly because some market participants with wind generation assets, 

recognizing the uncertainty of the wind forecast in day-ahead, offered a percent of the 

forecasted capacity or, in some cases, choose not to participate in the day-ahead market at all. 

This may cause other generation to clear in the day-ahead market that will not be needed in 

real-time when the wind replaces it. Reliability unit commitments also fill the gap left by virtual 

transactions. A virtual transaction is not tied to an obligation to generate or consume energy; 

rather, it is a financial instrument that is cleared by taking the opposite position in the real-time 

market. Because the reliability unit commitment processes must ensure sufficient physical 

generation is on-line to meet energy demand, virtual transactions are not included in the 

reliability unit commitment processes used in day-ahead, intra-day, or short-term. Finally, import 

and export transaction data are updated to include the latest information available for the 

reliability unit commitment processes.  

State of the Market 2024 

91 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

The resource gaps can help explain why some generators produce much less in real-time or why 

additional commitments occur after the day-ahead market has cleared. Figure 3–23 compares 

on-line capacity between the day-ahead and real-time markets alongside the average prices.  

Figure 3–23  Average hourly capacity increase from day-ahead to real-time 

The chart indicates that in 2024 there was, on average, around 4,363 MWs of additional 

dispatchable generation cleared incremental to the day-ahead market, an increase of 3% from 

2023 and a decrease of 3% compared to 2022. The spike in prices early in the year stem from 

the winter storm experienced in mid-January.  

Figure 3–24 shows that the resource gap compared across years. 

Figure 3–24  Average hourly capacity increase from day-ahead to real-time with wind 

and virtual components

As can be seen above, the main driver of the resource gap, the wind gap, has remained mostly 

flat since 2021 with a very slight increase in 2024. Over the last three years, the wind gap 

averaged around 2,900 megawatts. By contrast, the virtuals gap – the second largest contributor 

to the resource gap - decreased by 24% between 2022 and 2023 but rebounded to 93% of 2022 

levels in 2024.  

Figure 3–24 also demonstrates correlation between the shapes of the wind gap, virtuals gap, 

and the shape of the overall resource gap. This further indicates that the wind and virtuals gap 

are the primary driver of the total resource gap. As discussed in Section 2.8, virtuals occur mostly 

at wind generator locations. The effect of this is seen in Figure 3–24 as the shape of the virtual 

gap largely follows the shape of the wind gap. This, along with previous analysis done by the 

MMU, indicates that virtual transactions, at least in part, capitalize on the divergence caused by 

wind under offering in the day-ahead market. 

On average, the market-wide resource gap is positive, and no additional capacity is needed in 

real-time. However, in some cases, the day-ahead market can clear insufficient generation. This 

can be a case that is not represented by the average, a locational insufficiency due to 

congestion, or a parameter that is not directly or sufficiently cleared in the day-ahead market 

such as ramp. When the day-ahead market clears insufficient generation, additional capacity 

may be committed for reliability after day-ahead. 

  RAMPING 

One of the reasons for reliability commitments is the need for ramp capability. The increase or 

decrease of the resource’s output to achieve the next dispatch instruction is called “ramp.” The 

number of megawatts a resource can ramp in one minute is the resource’s “ramp rate.” 

The instantaneous load capacity constraint may commit additional resources to ensure there is 

adequate ramping capacity to meet the instantaneous peak demand for any given hour. The 

instantaneous load capacity constraint is defined as the greater of the forecasted instantaneous 

peak load, or an SPP defined default value. Figure 3–25 shows the percentage of hours for which 

the default value is used for the upper bound and lower bound of instantaneous load capacity.  

State of the Market 2024 

93 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

Figure 3–25  Frequency of minimum requirement for instantaneous load capacity 

A value is calculated for upper bound (upward ramp) and a lower bound (downward ramp) 

based on forecasted load. However, the default, or minimum requirement, is not based on 

market information. Because the default value is used in over half of all intervals, the 

instantaneous load capacity constraint can contribute to reliability commitments that are not 

based on current market information. The default requirements are hourly values as low as 200 

MW. SPP evaluates the default values quarterly. 

The percent of hours at various upper bound requirements is shown in Figure 3–26.  

Figure 3–26  Instantaneous load capacity upper bound requirements 

The most frequent observations were from 500 MW to 599 MW at around 23% of hours. This is 

consistent with 2023 numbers and represents a shift upward from 2022, which saw more 

observations of the head-room requirement fall between 300 MW and 499 MW. While a market-

State of the Market 2024 

94 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

based product is more appropriate for a market efficiency improvement, keeping this 

requirement low may help reduce unnecessary make-whole payments. A continued shift upward 

in the default requirement may diminish any possible benefit of lowering unnecessary make-

whole payments.  

Resources committed to provide ramp capability can affect real-time prices, whether because of 

applying the instantaneous load capacity constraint in a reliability unit commitment process or a 

manual process. Without the appropriate scarcity pricing rules that reflect the market value of 

capacity shortages due to ramp capability, the cost of bringing the resource on-line may not be 

fully reflected in the real-time prices. The resource preventing the market from being scarce may 

not be paid to provide the needed capability. Additionally, manual commitments made during 

conservative operations, while possibly needed for capacity, similarly suppress the price signals 

when they are needed most.  

Reliability commitments, along with wind exceeding the day-ahead forecast, can dampen real-

time price signals, as is evidenced by nearly 50% of real-time make-whole payments made for 
reliability unit commitments, as shown in Figure 4–27.54  

In real-time, resources are increasing and decreasing output to meet changes in both load and 

non-dispatchable generation. These changes can be measured as changes in net load. Net load 

is net of non-dispatchable generation, net imports/exports, and parallel flows from other 

markets. 

Figure 3–27 shows the frequency and extent of net load changes from one real-time interval to 

the next.

As variable energy resources serve more load, volatility is expected to increase. Variable energy 

resources, primarily wind and solar, produced about 34% of total generation in 2024, down 

slightly from 2023. This decrease in variable energy resource production explains much of the 

decrease in volatility from 2023 to 2024. Of all intervals where these resources were on-line and 

available for economic dispatch, variable energy resources were expected to follow dispatch in 

about 10% of resource-intervals, with forecasted or dispatchable variable energy resources 

expected to follow dispatch roughly 12% of resource-intervals. When these forecasted resources 

are not following dispatch, it is possible for them to reduce the ramp need when they move in 

the same direction as demand, provided their rate of change is roughly in line with the rate of 

change in demand. However, they can also increase the amount of ramp needed when they 

move in the opposite direction as demand their rate of change outpace that of demand by a 

large quantity. When they increase the system ramp need, flexible, dependable resources are 

needed to account for the additional ramp they cause.

undispatched forecasted resources decreased net ramp need by only about 130 to 175 MWs, 

averaging 153 MW across the year. Because the precise timing of the fluctuations of forecasted 

resources is unknown, rampable capacity must be committed and available throughout the day.

  SCARCITY PRICING 

A scarcity price is a price that reflects the value of a product when there is not enough of the 

product to meet the demand. SPP’s market uses marginal cost pricing, which prices a product by 

the cost to produce the next increment. When a product is scarce, there may not be additional 

supply, so price cannot be determined by the next increment. In this case, a scarcity price is used 

to set marginal price. The Integrated Marketplace uses demand curves to set graduated scarcity 

prices so that small scarcities are priced lower than large scarcities. Scarcity prices inform market 

participants that the product was short and incentivize future provision of that product. 

When an insufficient amount of ramp capability-up service, ramp capability-down service, 

regulation-up service, regulation-down service, or contingency reserve is cleared, a scarcity price 

is set by a demand curve. The scarcity of these products can be caused by a lack of capacity or a 

lack of ramp. Scarcities are due to capacity when there are insufficient resources at maximum 

output available to meet demand. Scarcities are due to ramp when sufficient capacity is 

available, but ramp rate limitations do not allow access to the full capacity. When multiple 

products compete for the same, limited capability of resources, the scarcity of one product can 

also raise the price of other products. As of May 2022, scarcity prices are set by the pricing run 
according to the dispatch in the pricing run.55 Scarcity or a lack of scarcity in the dispatch run 

has no bearing on scarcity prices in the marketplace. 

Regulation, ramp capability, and contingency reserve scarcities are priced by demand curves. 

The regulation demand curves, for both up and down, consist of six steps with a maximum price 

of $600/MW. The contingency reserve demand curve consists of three steps with a maximum 

price of $1,100/MW. The demand curve for ramp capability, for both up and down, consists of 

six steps with the maximum scarcity price is based on the average cost to run a dispatchable 

fast-start resource at its maximum output for its minimum run time. SPP updates this maximum 

demand curve price monthly based on three months of historical offers. The highest maximum 

ramp demand curve price for any month during 2024 was $32/MW.  

The clearing engine does not record the reason for the scarcity, (i.e. whether it is caused by a 

lack of capacity or ramp.) The MMU suggests that SPP captures the appropriate information so 

that the reason for the scarcity will be transparent. 

55 Pricing runs and dispatch runs are products of fast-start logic. 

State of the Market 2024

spikes for regulation-up, regulation-down, and contingency reserve happened more frequently 

at the beginning of each hour, while ramp capability-up scarcity peaked toward the end of the 

hour. Figure 3–32 below illustrates the average count of scarcity events in the real-time market
