Figure 3–32  Scarcity by product by interval, real-time 

About 16% of real-time regulation-up scarcities and about 48% of regulation-down scarcities 

occurred in the first interval of the hour. Regulation scarcity events typically happen at the 

beginning of the hour and have since the inception of the marketplace. Contingency reserve 

scarcities were more equally distributed across the hour, though they also peaked at 14% in the 

first interval of the hour. Ramp capability-up scarcities increase steadily as the hour progresses.  

One potential reason for regulation scarcities peaking at the beginning of the hour is that SPP 

does not pre-position regulation-providing resources to be within their regulating maximum 

and minimum limits prior to the period that the resource is cleared for regulation. Consider a 

resource that is currently dispatched to its minimum of 100 MW. If this resource clears 20 MW of 

regulation-down reserves in the next hour, it will need to move up to 120 MW. If the resource’s 

ramp rate does not allow it to ramp up 20 MW in one interval, the resource cannot provide 

regulation-down in the first interval. If this causes a scarcity event, the resource may have to buy 

back a day-ahead position at scarcity prices. However, if the resource moves there prior to the 
hour, it will deviate from its current dispatch instruction, which has financial penalties.56 

Consequently, resources often follow dispatch until the first interval of the regulation 

commitment, contributing to shortages in the first interval of the regulating commitment. 

The volume of scarcity events highlighted in Figure 3–32, illustrates the need for ramp capability. 

SPP’s ramp capability product was implemented in March 2022. While the MMU generally 

supported the proposed design, the MMU did have some concerns with the design prior to

figure has also trended downward from $44.92 in 2022 and $28.88 in 2023.

implementation.57 In addition to these concerns, the MMU identified an issue after 

implementation with the deliverability of ramp cleared by the ramp-up capability product. The 

MMU pre-implementation concerns and the deliverability concerns are both discussed later in 

this section.  

The MMU’s preliminary findings raise the following concerns with the performance of the ramp 

capability product: 

1.  Variable energy resources cannot produce regulation-up in the SPP market. However, 
71% of the day-ahead ramp-up product was cleared on variable energy resources, a 
sharp increase from 61% in 2023. This figure was 43% for real-time, up from 36% in 
2023. 

2.  The scarcity demand curve prices for the ramp capability up product may be set too low. 
There is a high frequency of ramp-up scarcity events, especially when taking into account 
the fact that non-deployable ramp-up is being cleared to meet the requirement. 

3.  The real-time dispatch does not consider future intervals. It simply calculates one value: a 

dispatch instruction for the next interval. While the real-time balancing market considers 

a resource’s ramp capability for the purpose of calculating the dispatch instruction for 

the next interval, ramp is not considered for any interval after that. Prior to the ramp 

product’s implementation, ramp needs were not accounted for in terms of the 

subsequent dispatch instructions even though ramp is the very capability that allows a 

resource to get to future dispatch instructions. 

4.  Not having ramp capability considered for future intervals can cause an inability in the 

market clearing engine to procure enough energy to serve the load or provide sufficient 

operating reserves in those future intervals. Even when enough capacity is available, a 

lack of ramp renders that capacity temporarily unreachable. Moreover, sufficient ramp 

has typically been offered by the market participants, but the clearing process has not 

left enough available for future use. When this occurs in the pricing run, it often leads to 
short-term transitory price spikes.58

just slightly more diverse with about 53% lasting for one interval, about 11% lasting two

recent years. For ramp-up scarcity events, 40% lasted one interval, and 16% lasted for two

events. There was one uncertainty scarcity event and it lasted one interval. 

Where sufficient capacity cannot be dispatched, scarcity prices are invoked. Scarcity prices are 

economic signals alerting market participants to the insufficient supply of a product. Almost all 

of these intervals with scarcity pricing were due to a lack of cleared ramp and not a lack of 

capacity. If the region reserved sufficient ramp for these scarcity intervals, then these scarcities 

likely could have been avoided. Ramp availability increased in previous years, but scarcity events 

have also increased, highlighting the continued need for systematic ramp procurement. The 

ramp capability product was expected to help reduce these transient scarcity events. 

In addition, marginal energy prices can be elevated even when energy is not scarce. When ramp 

in the up direction is short, energy will always be given the highest priority. If there is not 

sufficient ramp to meet both energy and regulation-up, for instance, then the regulation-up 

scarcity price will be reflected in the marginal energy price. This causes a high marginal energy 

price even though there is no energy scarcity because the two products are competing for ramp. 

This makes energy prices more volatile. If sufficient ramp had been available, then regulation-up 

scarcity prices would not have raised the marginal energy price. A well-designed ramp capability 

product can ensure that more rampable capacity is available to meet energy so that regulation-

up scarcity prices can be avoided. This helps to better reflect system conditions and reduces 

dispatch volatility.  

  ENERGY ATTRIBUTES 

  Ramp 

In the 2017 annual report, the market monitor recommended that SPP create a ramp capability 

product. SPP implemented the ramp capability product in March 2022. This product is used to 

provision rampable up and down capacity for uncertainty events in net load forecasts across a 

10-minute future time horizon. Historically, SPP operators procured ramp through out-of-

market mechanisms, often resulting in uplift payments. 

The ramp capability product was designed to allow for more economical and transparent 

management of the intermittent aspects of the system. The ramp capability product design 

optimizes the resources’ dispatch instructions over a ten-minute period to allocate any 

State of the Market 2024 

104 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

economically available ramp for the interval starting ten minutes in the future. Future ramp 

needs get met by pre-positioning online resources with available ramp if the cost of this action 

is less than the applicable ramp-scarcity demand curve price. The ramp requirement is set to 

procure enough ramp to meet forecasted net load changes plus an amount to cover unexpected 

net load changes based on historical needs. The ramp product optimizes only online ramp. Off-

line ramp is not eligible to clear the ramp capability product, due to the short 10-minute 

clearing horizon. A market-clearing price will be set by the opportunity cost of providing other 

products.  

Figure 3–35 and Figure 3–36 below show the day-ahead and real-time cleared ramp-up 

megawatt hours for each product by fuel type.  

Figure 3–35  Cleared ramp-up by resource type, day-ahead 

State of the Market 2024 

105 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

Figure 3–36  Cleared ramp-up by resource type, real-time 

As shown in the two above charts a large percentage of ramp capability-up is cleared by wind 

and natural gas resources. These figures also show that wind resources clear a greater 

percentage in the day-ahead market than the real-time market. Seventy-one percent of the 

ramp capability-up cleared in the day-market was cleared by wind, as compared to only 43% 

cleared by wind resources in real time. 

In winter 2022, the MMU evaluated the effectiveness of the newly implemented ramp product 

and discovered a design issue. This issue along with the market monitor’s other pre-
implementation concerns are discussed below:60 

Ramp capability-up procured behind binding constraints 

The MMU was concerned with the trend of day-ahead ramp capability up scarcity events being 

roughly two and a half times the frequency of real-time ramp capability up scarcity events. This 

is not a logical trend as there is far less uncertainty in the day-ahead market than the real-time 

market. In the day-ahead market, resources are considered to always stay on dispatch, 

forecasted outputs are assumed to be in line with forecasted quantities, and resources are not 

considered to trip. These assumptions do not hold true in the real-time market, so typically 

there are far more regulation and operating reserve shortages due to ramp limitations in real-

time than day-ahead. 

The reason for the disparity between day-ahead and real-time ramp shortages has to do with 

how ramp capability-up is accessed for clearing. The resource owners do not offer a cost for 

either ramp capability-up or -down. Instead, the products clear solely based off their 

60 See MMU comments in Docket No. ER20-1617. 

State of the Market 2024 

106 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

opportunity costs for not clearing other products, such as energy. For instance, if a resource had 

an energy locational marginal price of $35/MWh and marginal energy cost of $30/MWh, the 

opportunity cost for ramp capability-up would be $5/MWh. This means that the price for ramp 

capability-up will need to be at least $5/MWh for this resource to clear the product. However, all 

dispatchable resource types with available ramp may participate in clearing this product, and 

congestion is not considered in the clearing. This means that a resource behind a constraint may 

have a −$65/MWh energy locational marginal price and marginal energy cost of $10/MWh. In 

this case, this resources opportunity cost would be −$55/MWh. The market-clearing engine 

considers all negative opportunity cost to have $0/MWh opportunity costs.  

There is a disconnect with the assumptions used in the clearing of the ramp capability product 

and the actual deployment of the ramp. When the market clearing engine goes to deploy the 

ramp cleared behind the constraint, it is often too expensive to meet the ramp needs in the 

market. This can make the market go short of products, when the cost of congestion relief is 

higher than the scarcity demand curves. In the example above, the −$55/MWh opportunity cost 

will likely get more negative as the resource gets dispatched up for ramp. When the costs to 

ramp-up the resource get higher than the scarcity or violation relaxation limits the market will 

go scarce the product and price it with the associated scarcity price. Ramp capability scarcity 

demand curves are discussed in Section 3.2.3.  

Figure 3–37 below shows the megawatts of ramp capability-up cleared on resources with shift 

factors on a binding or breached constraint in the real-time market in 2024. 

Figure 3–37  Real-time cleared ramp-up effect on constraints 

The above graph illustrates that there is a large percentage of ramp capability-up being cleared 

behind a binding or breached constraint in the real-time market. The negative values represent 

State of the Market 2024 

107 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

resources with shift factors that increase congestion on a constraint, thus decreasing the ability 

to deliver ramp-up to the market. This is a leading contributor to the market not being able to 

use the ramp procured. SPP is working on a potential solution to reduce the effects of stranding 

ramp behind constraints.  

Ramp product demand curve prices are likely too low 

The ramp product prices scarcity with a demand curve. The MMU had concerns that the demand 

curve prices for ramp are likely too low. Prior to implementation, there was a concern that low 

prices may not allow the market to provision rampable capacity even though it is available. 

Consequently, physical ramp may be insufficient, and the price may not reflect the actual value 

of ramp, which undermines the purpose of a ramp product.  

The MMU recommended that the maximum demand curve price be set slightly below the 

minimum regulation demand curve price. Avoiding regulation scarcity events in the future is the 

primary goal of a ramp product. A higher scarcity price may be needed to clear physical ramp 

and to incentivize ramp capability. The MMU reviewed the ramp-up and ramp-down scarcity 

demand curve’s effectiveness. Ramp-down has had a $0/MW market clearing price since 

implementation in March 2022. This is due to the abundance of rampable down capacity 

available in the market.  

As stated before, ramp capability-up did have scarcity in both day-ahead and real-time markets. 

However, the MMU was unable to analyze the effects of the ramp-up demand curves, due to the 

issues discussed in the previous section concerning the deliverability of the procured ramp 

capability-up. In order to assess the effectiveness of the demand curves, we recommend that 

SPP staff evaluate the effectiveness of the demand curves when assessing solutions to the 

stranded ramping issue. There were no concerns with ramp capability-down scarcity demand 
curves as the product has never gone scarce in either the day-ahead or real-time markets.61  

Reduced need for instantaneous load capacity 

The process known as instantaneous load capacity is ramp procurement without ramp payment. 

The instantaneous load capacity ensures that sufficient rampable capacity is committed to ramp 

from one average hourly load to the next. Resources committed to provide this rampable 

capacity add value to the market but are not paid for that value. These resources often run at a 

financial loss for most of the hour and are merely made whole to their costs. The instantaneous 

load capacity requirement is not removed or reduced by the proposed ramp product. If more 

61 See Figures 3-31 and 3-34. 

State of the Market 2024 

108 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

than one ramping timeframe is needed for more than one ramping purpose,62 then the market 

monitor could support multiple ramp products. 

SPP implemented an uncertainty product in July 2023 that works similarly to the ramp product, 
with a one-hour time horizon, and off-line resources are able to participate.63 The MMU believes 

that this product, in conjunction with the ramp capability product, should significantly reduce 

the need for the use of committing the uncompensated capacity, currently defined as 

instantaneous load capacity. 

  Uncertainty 

SPP implemented an uncertainty product on July 6, 2023. The product’s objective is similar to 

the ramp product in that it was designed to reorganize the market dispatch to efficiently ensure 

rampable capacity for future needs, while effectively pricing and paying for the procured 

rampable capacity. There are two main differences between the uncertainty product and the 

ramp product, which was implemented in 2022. First, the ramp product has a 10 minute 

assessment horizon while the uncertainty product has a one-hour horizon, for which they can 

both procure ramp. Second, the uncertainty product allows clearing from offline and online 

resources, while the ramp product only clears from online resources. Online resources clear 

based off opportunity costs, just as described in 3.2.5 for the ramp product, where offline 

resources provide competitive offers.  

Prices for the uncertainty product have remained low. Figure 3–38, below, displays the average 

monthly uncertainty market clearing prices (MCP) for both day-ahead and real-time. 

62 For instance, it may be appropriate to have a ramp product similar to instantaneous load capacity to 
address inter-hour ramping needs in addition to an intra-hour ramp product to address short-term load 
variability.  
63 See RR449, FERC Docket No. ER22-914.

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

Figure 3–38  Day-ahead and real-time average uncertainty market clearing prices 

Day-ahead uncertainty product market clearing prices averaged $0.08/MWh in 2024, up from 

the $0.02/Mwh average of 2023. Real-time prices averaged even lower at $0.10/MWh in 2024 

while 2023 had just one interval priced at $2.00/MWh.  

There was an uptick in prices that started in October 2024. This was primarily the result of a 

change to how the requirement for the uncertainty product is set. The MMU pointed out a 

concern in the 2023 Annual State of the Market Report that was believed to be excessivelly 
deflating the product’s required megawatts. 64 The requirement for the uncertainty product prior 

to October 2024 was being set by looking at the forecasted net-load increase over the 
uncertainty horizon then adding the 97th percentile of the forecast errors from the prior twelve 

months. This derived amount is called the flexibility requirement. Once the flexiblity requirement 

was set; the ramp capability-up, regulation-up, and contingency reserve requirements were 

backed out. Backing these other requirements out frequently led to the uncertainty product’s 

requirement to be set at zero, which also set the price a zero dollars. The Market Working Group 

voted in May 2024 to update the requirement to only back out the the ramp capability-up 
product’s requirement from the flexibility requirement. 65 This lead to an uptick in cleared 

megawatts and prices.

online resources.

Soon-to-start resources are resources expected to start during the uncertainty horizon. These 

resources provide ramp to the market, but are not paid through settlements, nor are they able 

to set price as their opportunity costs is considered to be zero dollars. In 2024, 26% of the total 

day-ahead uncertainty clearing was meet with soon-to-start resources compared to just 20% of 

the real-time clearing. The MMU made recommendations in last year’s annual state of the

market report that SPP review the use of soon-to-start resources in meeting the requirement for 

uncertainty, as soon-to-start are not used to meet any other SPP product requirements. SPP has 

opted to hold off on reviewing soon-to-start until the effects from the uncertainty requirement 

changes have had ample time to be observed.

4  MARKET PRICES AND COSTS 

This chapter covers market prices and costs in the SPP market, along with related metrics on 

negative prices, make-whole payments, and long-run price signals for investment. Highlights of 

this chapter include:  

•  Average gas price for 2024 at the Panhandle Eastern hub was $1.81/MMBtu, down 16% 

from $2.16/MMBtu in 2023. 

•  The average hourly day-ahead price decreased by 3% from $28.45/MWh in 2023 to 

$27.56/MWh in 2024 and the real-time price decreased by 4% from $27.39/MWh in 2023 

to $26.18/MWh in 2024.  

• 

In 2024, 9.9% of all asset owner intervals in the day-ahead market had prices below zero; 

this is up from 6.3% of all intervals in 2023. In the real-time market, the frequency of 

negative price intervals was 15.2% of all intervals in 2024, up from 11.8% in 2023 

•  For 2024, day-ahead market and reliability unit commitment make-whole payments 

combined totaled $257 million. Reliability unit commitment make-whole payments 

constituted 54% of the total make-whole payments in 2024. Day-ahead make-whole 

payments were over $119 million in 2024, up from $79 million in 2023, an increase of 

51%. Reliability unit commitment make-whole payments in 2024 totaled $138 million, 

down from $157 million in 2023, representing a 12% decrease. 

• 

In 2024, 68 resources received over $1 million in total make-whole payments, an increase 

from 62 in 2023. The highest payment to a single resource in 2024 was nearly $14 

million, though it was the only resource to receive over $10 million.  

•  On a market participant level, nine market participants each received annual make-whole 

payments in excess of $10 million. These nine market participants accounted for 75% of 

the total make-whole payments paid out in 2024. 

•  Total monthly revenue neutrality uplift for 2024 was $163 million, down 21% from $205 

million in 2023. The real-time joint operating agreement portion of revenue neutrality 

uplift was just over $112 million in 2024, an increase of 20% from $94 million in 2023. 

The real-time congestion and revenue inadequacy components of revenue neutrality 

uplift was $257 million in 2024, a decrease of 9% from $283 million in 2023 

•  Historically, market revenues alone have been insufficient to support the cost of new 

entry for all major generation technologies since the start of the Integrated Marketplace. 

Exceptions to this have been in 2021, due to increased revenue due to the February 

State of the Market 2024 

113 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Market prices and costs 

winter storm and in 2022 for certain wind resources. In 2024, this broader trend largely 

continued. However, combined-cycle and combustion turbine generation in the SPS 

region were able to recover their cost of new entry due to a glut of wet gas production 

at the Waha trading hub that resulted in natural gas prices below zero for much of the 

year.

On a monthly basis in 2024, natural gas prices were highest in January at $3.21/MMBtu and

Market prices and costs 

Historically, on-peak prices tend to average about $15/MWh higher than off-peak prices, in both 

day-ahead and real-time. However, in 2022, the average spread between on-peak and off-peak 

prices grew to a difference of $22/MWh. The spread dropped back to the $15/MWh range in 

2023, and to the $10-$12/MWh range in 2024. Summer months typically experience the largest 

spread in both the day-ahead and real-time, approaching $20/MWh in 2024. The differences 

between on-peak and off-peak prices can be mostly attributed to lower loads and a higher 

percentage of lower cost wind generation in off-peak hours. 

Changes in gas prices have historically had the highest impact on electricity prices compared to 

other fuels. This is because the short-run marginal costs of coal-fired generation historically 

have been cheaper than natural gas-fired generation; hence, gas units are most frequently 

marginal. The energy pricing continued to trend with the gas prices throughout the year. Figure 

4–4 compares various fuel price indices with real-time prices.

Market prices and costs 

This figure shows that regional natural gas prices continuously dropped from 2023 to 2024 on 

an annual basis. The Southern Star gas price averaged $1.88/MMBtu for 2024, while the 

Panhandle Eastern Pipeline averaged $1.84/MMBtu, and the Henry Hub was $2.19/MMBtu for 
2024.68 Henry Hub average prices were slightly higher than Southern Star and Panhandle Eastern 

average prices in 2024. Price differences between Henry Hub and Panhandle Eastern continued 

to trend closely, averaging $0.58/MMBtu in 2022, $0.38/MMBtu in 2023 and $0.34/MMBtu. This 

difference is likely driven by pipeline constraints in the Texas and Oklahoma area. Often, natural 

gas is a byproduct of oil drilling. Natural gas production has continued to outpace takeaway 

capacity in this area, with incremental production volumes quickly inundating any available 

space in the pipelines and keeping supply-area prices at discounts compared to other hubs.  

Coal prices have remained relatively stable since 2016 but saw an increase in 2022. The trend 
reversed in 2024, back to the same level as 2023.69 The price for 8,400 Btu/lb. at the Powder 

River Basin decreased from $0.71/MMBtu in 2023 to $0.67/MMBtu (down 5%) in 2024, and the 

2024 price for 8,800 Btu/lb. was $0.82/MMBtu, five cents below the 2023 average. In 2022, high 

gas prices, supply-side constraints, and energy security concerns drove coal prices to all-time 

highs. Lower gas prices and greater coal supply led to a pullback in coal prices in 2023 and 2024.

energy cost – a result of low natural gas and coal prices.

Market prices and costs 

SPP has two pricing hubs: the SPP North hub and the SPP South hub. The SPP North hub 

represents a portion of pricing nodes in the northern part of the SPP footprint, generally in 

Nebraska. The SPP South hub represents a portion of pricing nodes in the south-central portion 

of the footprint, generally in central Oklahoma. Figure 4–6 shows the average day-ahead prices 

and real-time prices at the two SPP market hubs. 

Figure 4–6  Hub prices, day-ahead and real-time 

The general pattern of higher prices in the south and lower in the north is primarily due to fuel 

mix and congestion. Coal, nuclear, and wind are the dominant fuels in the north and west. The 

south and east have a much larger share of gas generation, hence, more exposure to changes in 

fuel prices. However, February and March 2024 saw South hub prices slightly below North hub 

prices. This can primarily be attributed to congestion around North hub settlement locations, 

along with low gas prices. After a spread in the $15/MWh range for 2022 between the North 

and South hubs in both the day-ahead and real-time markets, the spread decreased to around 

$2/MWh 2023, then increased to around $9/MWh in 2024. The increased separation in 2022 

coincides with the increase in natural gas costs and congestion during the year. Also of note is 

that October 2024 saw the largest monthly spread in prices between the North and South hubs 

since the start of the Integrated Marketplace at around $33/MWh. The congestion pattern 

during October contributed to increased price divergence between the hubs.

16, 17, 18, then gradually returning to the narrow spread as the day comes to a close. This larger 

spread during peak hours shows that higher prices at the south hub are driven by higher priced 

gas generation in the south part of the SPP footprint.

  ENERGY PRICE VOLATILITY  

Price volatility73 in the SPP market is shown in Figure 4–9 below. As expected, day-ahead prices 

are much less volatile than those in real-time. The day-ahead market does not experience the 

actual (unexpected) congestion and changes in load or generation found in the real-time 

market. Real-time volatility tends to peak in the spring and fall, roughly corresponding with 

times of higher wind and lower load but can also peak during the summer months because of 

peak load conditions. 

Figure 4–9  System price volatility 

In 2024, volatility in the day-ahead market increased from 2023, while volatility in the real-time 

market was essentially flat. The winter weather event in January resulted in the highest volatility 

in prices for the year. 

  DAY-AHEAD AND REAL-TIME PRICE CONVERGENCE 

Price convergence between day-ahead and real-time prices is important, because the more day-

ahead prices reflect real-time prices, the better unit commitment and positioning of resources 

occurs for real-time operations. 

73 Volatility is calculated as the standard deviation for prices received by load-serving entities in the SPP 
market. The standard deviation is calculated using hourly price in the day-ahead market and interval (five 
minute) price in the real-time market.  

State of the Market 2024 

121 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Market prices and costs 

While average prices in the day-ahead and real-time markets have been close over the past 

several years, average prices can mask real-time volatility and underlying price differences. The 

averaging of price spikes, and in particular, high prices during periods of scarcity, drove real-

time average prices up, closer to day-ahead prices. These short-term, transient price spikes can 
be attributed to limitations in ramping capability.74 

In this section, underlying differences in prices after controlling for scarcity events are 

highlighted. This analysis shows that a significant volume of generation, particularly from wind 

resources, not cleared in the day-ahead market, drives down real-time prices. 

Many factors cause prices to diverge between the day-ahead and real-time markets. Some of 

these factors may include, but are not limited to: 

•  Day-ahead offers may include premiums to account for uncertainty in real-time fuel 

prices or forced outage risk.75  

•  Load and wind forecast errors can cause differences in the real-time market results.  

•  Participants may not bid in all load or offer all generation in the day-ahead market.  

•  Modeling differences including transmission outages between the two markets.  

•  Generation outages or derates that were different in real-time than was anticipated in 

the day-ahead.  

• 

Impacts from other RTOs that were not anticipated may affect the SPP real-time market. 

•  Changes in imports and exports from other systems in the real-time markets. 

•  Unanticipated weather changes. 

Price divergence76 between the day-ahead and real-time markets, at the system level, is shown 

in Figure 4–10 below. Market participants may be willing to pay a premium for price certainty in 

the day-ahead market. This can result in higher prices in the day-ahead market. A large 

divergence between the day-ahead and real-time prices may indicate that actual conditions in 

the market do not match the expected conditions. An extended period of a large variance 

74 For further information on ramping issues, see Section 3.2.5.

development of mitigated energy offers. 
76 Price divergence is calculated as the difference between day-ahead and real-time prices, using system 
prices for each five-minute (real-time) or hour (day-ahead) interval. The absolute divergence is calculated 
by taking the absolute value of the divergence for each interval.

between day-ahead and real-time prices may indicate a structural or design deficiency in the 

market. 

Figure 4–10  Price divergence 

Average absolute divergence for 2024 was $13.37/MWh, up slightly from $12.69/MWh in 2023, 

but down from $19.89/MWh in 2022. Historically, absolute divergence has averaged around $10 

per month up through 2020. The under-clearing of renewable resources and short-term 

ramping limitations contribute to increased price divergence. It was hoped that implementation 

of the ramping product on March 1, 2022, would also have a positive effect on this divergence; 

however, the impact has been negligible to date. The MMU recommends that additional work 

be done to improve price divergence related to under-clearing of renewable generation in the 
day-ahead market.77

The marginal energy cost is one of three components that factor into locational marginal prices 

and represents the marginal cost to provide the next increment of dispatch, absent losses and 

congestion. Day-ahead prices are generally at a premium when compared to real-time prices 

(excluding scarcity pricing), particularly in the off-peak hours. Also, the marginal energy prices 

follow the same monthly trend as gas prices.

Wind generation made up 71% of the 4,428 MW of incremental real-time generation in 2024 

(up slightly from 70% in 2023 and the same as 2022), with an hourly average of 3,155 MW of 

additional generation in real-time. Self-committed generation accounted for an additional 380 

MW and reliability unit committed or manually committed generation averaged 636 MW. While 

SPP is a net exporter in both the day-ahead and real-time markets on average, it sees an 

average hourly increase of 256 MW in real-time market net imports compared to the day-ahead. 

Averaging 2,802 MW an hour, net virtual positions helped to offset the additional generation, 

accounting for about 63% of the difference for the year. This is much higher than 1,462 MW in

net supply in real-time in 2024, compared to 2,476 MW in 2023.

In 2024, 83% of the wind offered in the day-ahead cleared. This is down from 86% in 2023 and 

88% in 2022. In 2023, 88% of forecasted wind was offered into the day-ahead, which is well 

below the 92% in 2023 and 102% in 2022. However, even though wind resources are generally 

offering in close to full forecasted capacity in the day-ahead, a portion of this is at offer levels 

that exceed prevailing prices and thus does not clear the market. Typically, wind will clear all the 

megawatts that are physically offered into the day-ahead market if the economic offers are 

consistent with real-time offers. The MMU observed that almost all wind megawatts offered into 

the day-ahead market and not cleared had higher economic offers in the day-ahead market 

than real-time. 

Systematic under-clearing of wind resources in the day-ahead market can contribute to 

distorted price signals, suppressed real-time prices, and insufficient revenue adequacy. Variable 

energy resources are generally able to produce close to a forecasted amount. Therefore, the 

MMU continues to recommend that SPP and its stakeholders address this issue through market 

incentives and rule changes that focus on market inefficiencies associated with under-clearing of 

variable energy resources in the day-ahead market based on forecasted supply. These rule 

changes could focus on changing incentives for wind resources or alternatively reducing barriers 

for virtual transactions. 

  NEGATIVE PRICES 

With the prolific growth of wind generation in the SPP market, the incidence of intervals with 

negative prices continues to grow. While the years 2021 through 2023 saw a downward trend in 

day-ahead negative pricing intervals, 2024 saw a notable uptick in negative pricing intervals. The 

State of the Market 2024 

126 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Market prices and costs 

increase in negative pricing intervals from 2023 to 2024 may have been driven in part by an 

increase in wind generation of around 6.5% from 2023 to 2024. 

Figure 4–15  Negative price intervals, day-ahead, monthly 

In 2024, 9.9% of all asset owner intervals79 in the day-ahead market had prices below zero, as 

shown in Figure 4–15. This is up from 6.3% of all intervals in 2023. April again had the highest 

percentage of negative intervals in the day-ahead market, at 25.6%. This is significantly higher 

than April of 2023, which had negative day-ahead pricing in 15% of intervals. 

Historically, negative price intervals in the real-time market occur around two times more 

frequently than in the day-ahead market.

per day * 30 days).

−$0.01 MWh and −$25 MWh and only 3% of intervals with negative prices having values below

As shown above, negative price intervals in the real-time market occur more frequently than in

At the asset owner level (for those serving load), the distribution of negative price intervals

Figure 4–19  Negative price intervals, real-time, by asset owner 

In 2024, 31 of 71 asset owners (44%) with load experienced negative prices in more than 15% of 

total intervals. This is an increase from 2023, where only 27% of asset owners experienced 

negative prices in excess of 15% of total intervals. The asset owner with negative prices the 

highest percentage of intervals with negative prices in 2024 had 34% of intervals with negative 

prices – up from 2023 when the highest percentage was 29% of all intervals. 

The MMU remains vigilant regarding the frequency of negative price intervals. Negative prices 

may not be a problem in and of themselves, however, they do indicate an increase in surplus 

energy on the system. This is exacerbated by the practice of self-committing of resources and 

manual commitments for capacity. In the SPP market, where there is an abundance of total 

system capacity and significant levels of renewable resources, negative prices can occur. In this 

instance, renewable resources will need to be backed down in order for conventional resources 

to meet their committed generation. Moreover, unit commitment differences, due to under-

clearing of wind resources in the day-ahead market and then producing more in the real-time 

market, can create differences in the frequency of negative price intervals between the day-

ahead and real-time markets. This disparity between the markets negatively impacts the efficient 

commitment of resources.  

State of the Market 2024 

130 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Market prices and costs 

As more renewables are anticipated to be added over the coming years, the frequency of 

negative prices has the potential to increase. Negative price intervals in the day-ahead highlight 

the need for changes in market rules to address self-committing of resources in the day-ahead 

market and the differences in supply between day-ahead and real time. These issues are 

discussed further in Chapter 7. 

  OPERATING RESERVE MARKET PRICES  

Operating reserve is made up of four products: (1) regulation-up, (2) regulation-down, (3) 

spinning reserve, and (4) supplemental reserve. The regulation products are used to ensure the 

amount of generation matches load on a subinterval basis. Generators can respond to 

regulation instructions within cycles. Spinning and supplemental products are reserved for 

contingency situations and can respond to instructions within 10 minutes. 

In addition, regulating units are compensated for mileage costs incurred when moving from one 

set point instruction to another. Each month, the market calculates a mileage factor for both 

products (regulation-up mileage and regulation-down mileage), which represents the 

percentage a unit is expected to be deployed compared to what it cleared. If a unit is deployed 

more than the expected percentage, the unit will be entitled to reimbursement for the excess at 

the regulation mileage marginal price. If the unit is deployed less, it must buy back its position 

at the real-time mileage clearing price. 

Also of note is the sharp drop in regulation mileage prices starting in May 2024. The MMU had 

identified that increased regulation mileage prices are related, in part, to issues related to an 
outstanding MMU recommendation related to regulation mileage.80 Revision Request 504 (RR 

504) was passed by the Market Working Group, then approved by the Market and Operations 

Policy Committee (MOPC) in October 2022. The goal of RR 504 is to help reduce the occurrence 

of high mileage offers setting price. RR 504 does two things: 

1.  Mileage factor is currently calculated using the instructed mileage, which overstates the 

amount of regulation mileage that is seen on the system. This revision request proposes 

to change the calculation for the mileage factor to be the actual regulation mileage 

divided by the cleared regulation, instead of the instructed mileage divided by the 

cleared regulation. 

2.  Currently the mileage market clearing price (MCP) is set as the highest mileage price 

offered for resources that have cleared regulation. This revision request also proposes to 

change the way the mileage market clearing price is set – from the highest offered 

80 SPP MMU 2021 Annual State of the Market report, page 273. 

State of the Market 2024 

131 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Market prices and costs 

mileage price of cleared resource to the resource projected to provide the last mile, 

based on the mileage factor. 

The change was filed with FERC81 and was approved on June 14, 2023, with implementation 

scheduled for June 4, 2024. While this change was unintentionally implemented a month earlier 

than the FERC approved date, the intended effect of reducing high mileage offers setting price 

appears to have been successful, with regulation mileage prices dropping into the $2/MW to 

$4/MW range since implementation. 

Generally, regulation-up and regulation-down usually have the highest market clearing prices. 

Supplemental reserves always have the lowest average prices of the operating reserve products, 

with prices typically averaging less than two dollars on an annual basis.  

Day-ahead and real-time price patterns vary for regulation-up and regulation-down, see Figure 

4–20 and Figure 4–21.

$12/MW for 2024, up slightly from $11/MW for both in 2023. The regulation-up mileage price 

for 2024 was $7/MW, down from $12/MW in 2023. As stated above, the decrease of the 

regulation-up mileage price into the $2/MW to $4/MW range starting in May was due to the 

implementation of RR 504.

4.2  UPLIFT 

The Integrated Marketplace provides make-whole payments to generators to ensure that the 

market provides sufficient revenue to cover the cleared offers providing energy and operating 

reserves for the period in which the resource was committed. To preserve the incentive for a 

resource to meet its market commitment and dispatch instruction, market payments should 

cover the sum of the incremental energy cost, start-up cost, no-load cost, transition cost, and 

cost of operating reserve products. The make-whole payment provides additional market 

payments in cases where revenue is below a resource’s offer, to make the resource whole to its 

offer. 

For the resources that are not combined-cycle, settlements separately evaluate: (1) day-ahead 

market commitments based on day-ahead market prices and cleared offers; and (2) reliability 

unit commitments based on real-time market prices and cleared operating reserve offers. 

Combined-cycle resources that are registered as multi-configuration resources are unique in 

that they can be cleared in both the day-ahead and real-time markets at the same time. As a 

result, settlements must evaluate the revenues and cost of both real-time and day-ahead 

commitments when calculating real-time make-whole payments for combined-cycles.  

State of the Market 2024 

134 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

  MAKE-WHOLE PAYMENTS

For 2024, day-ahead market and reliability unit commitment make-whole payments combined 

totaled $257 million. Reliability unit commitment make-whole payments constituted 54% of the 

total make-whole payments in 2024.  

State of the Market 2024 

135 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Market prices and costs 

Day-ahead make-whole payments were over $119 million in 2024, up from $79 million in 2023, 

an increase of 51%. Reliability unit commitment make-whole payments in 2024 totaled $138 

million, down from $157 million in 2023, representing a 12% decrease. 

2024 saw a notable increase in make-whole payments to the day-ahead market, particularly for 

simple-cycle gas resources for the month of January. This is due to colder temperatures driving 

up the price of gas during extreme winter events seen around the footprint during this time 

frame causing increased make-whole payments to supplement the fuel costs. 

Some causes of make-whole payments included but are not limited to: local reliability 

commitments, congestion not accurately modeled in the day-ahead market, under-cleared 

generation (primarily wind) in the day-ahead market, inflexibility of resources to move in 

economic ranges or go offline between on-peak and off-peak hours, and excessive transmission 

congestion not being solved by physical resources. 

Total make-whole payments per megawatt generated averaged $0.88/MWh for 2024, up from 

$0.83/MWh for 2023, and significantly higher than $0.08/MWh in 2022. Self-committed 

resources are not eligible for make-whole payment reimbursements. Primarily, resources 

committed under reliability or market status are eligible for cost reimbursement.  

Figure 4–26 illustrates the 2022 to 2024 average make-whole payments per each megawatt 

eligible for cost reimbursement, or in other words those megawatts generated under either 

market or reliability status. These include manual commitments made by operators.

Day-ahead values for 2024 were larger than those for 2023 but were slightly less when looking 

at the real-time market. Values for 2024, for both day-ahead and real-time, were significantly 

lower than 2022, primarily due to higher natural gas prices in 2022.  

Operators often commit resources when the available ramp capacity needs in future intervals 

are perceived to be short. These actions often reduce the occurrence of scarcity events. 

However, this has the effect of potentially suppressing the price signal that would indicate a 

problem as capacity is brought on to meet the perceived ramp shortage. Manual commitments 

can and sometimes do lead to make-whole payments. Commitments that come from the 

State of the Market 2024 

136 

 
  
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Market prices and costs 

security-constrained economic unit commitment are preferred. Additionally, capacity committed 

manually can often lead to price suppression. Another way to view the real-time make-whole 

payments is that on average, $26.52/MWh was paid to avoid reliability problems that were not 

able to be addressed directly by the real-time market. 

In addition, most scarcity events last less than two intervals and most resources have start times 

that are longer than this period and minimum-run times that are much longer than this period. 

This means that even if these resources can capture one to two intervals of the high prices, they 

may have to run an hour or two longer with less economic price levels, leading to the need for 

cost reimbursement.  

The MMU believed that the ramp capability product (see Section 3.2.7.1), which went into 

production in March 2022, the uncertainty product (see Section 3.2.7.2) implemented in July 

2023, and fast-start resource pricing logic should help provide the appropriate pricing and 

compensation mechanisms for meeting ramp capacity needs in the market. However, both the 

ramp capability product and the uncertainty product need to be enhanced and improved to 

provide maximum benefit to the market. This includes reducing make-whole-payments and 

bringing transparency to the market. Enhancements to these products will also improve price 

formation and better compensate resources that provide the much-needed ramping flexibility, 

as well as reduce the need for manual commitments for capacity.

  MAKE-WHOLE PAYMENT ALLOCATION 

The allocation of both day-ahead and real-time make-whole payments has important 

consequences for the market. In principle, for market efficiency purposes, uplift cost allocation 

should be directed to those members that contributed to the need for the make-whole 

payments (i.e., cost causation). 

For the day-ahead market, make-whole payment costs are distributed to both physical and 

virtual withdrawals on a per-MWh rate. The per-MWh rate is derived by dividing the sum of all 

day-ahead make-whole payments for an operating day by the sum of all cleared day-ahead 

market load megawatts, export megawatts, and virtual bids for the operating day. The average 

per-MWh rate for withdrawing locations in the day-ahead market was $0.34/MWh for 2024; up 

from the 2023 rate of $0.26/MWh, but down from the 2022 rate of $0.56/MWh.  

For the real-time market, make-whole payment costs are distributed through a per-MWh rate 

that is assigned to all megawatt-hours of deviation in the real-time market. The 2024 per-MWh 

rate was $1.76/MWh, which is down from the $2.35/MWh seen in 2023, but substantially lower 

than the 2022 rate of $3.67/MWh. There are eight categories of deviation, and each category 

State of the Market 2024 

140 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Market prices and costs 

receives an equal amount per megawatt – which can vary by operating day when the cost of 

make-whole payments is applied.

time make-whole payment uplift charges for each deviation category.

Even though each category of deviation is applied the same rate for deviation, approximately 

84% (settlement location deviation in the table above) of the real-time make-whole payment 

costs were paid by entities withdrawing (physical or virtual) more megawatts in the real-time 

market than the day-ahead market.  

Transactions susceptible to this charge are virtual offer megawatts; real-time load megawatts 

different from the day-ahead megawatts cleared; import, export, and through megawatts in 

real-time different from the megawatts cleared in the day-ahead market; and units pulling 

substation power different from any megawatts produced by the unit. However, virtual offers 

are the most susceptible as 100% of their megawatts are considered incremental. Due to this, 

virtual offers alone paid 53% of all real-time make-whole payments in 2024, up from 48% in 

2023. Historically, virtual transactions have paid around 50% of real-time make-whole payments 

annually. 

Cost causation has been an area of concern in the SPP working groups in the past few years. 

Participants raised concerns that the market is not properly allocating the market cost back to 

those responsible for causing those costs. With virtual offers bearing such a heavy burden of 

these costs, it reduces the incentives for behavior changes among those that are causing the 

State of the Market 2024 

141 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Market prices and costs 

cost, and it adds a premium to virtual transactions. This should be considered as part of the 

evaluation of under-clearing of wind in the day-ahead as these incentives likely contribute to the 

lack of price convergence between day-ahead and real-time. 

  REGULATION MILEAGE MAKE-WHOLE PAYMENTS 

In March 2015, SPP introduced regulation compensation changes for units deployed for 

regulation-up and regulation-down. One component of the regulation compensation charges is 

regulation-up and regulation-down mileage make-whole payments for units that are charged 

for unused regulation-up or regulation-down mileage at a rate that is in excess of the 

regulation-up or regulation-down mileage offer.  

SPP calculates mileage factors monthly for both regulation-up and regulation-down. These 

mileage factors are ratios of historical averages of the percentage of each regulation product 

deployed to the regulation product cleared in the prior month. The regulation-up mileage factor 

averaged 12% in 2024, down from 14% in 2023, while the regulation-down mileage factor 

averaged 14% in 2024, up from 18% in 2023. 

The mileage factor is a key component in the computation of mileage make-whole payments. 

When the mileage factor is greater than the percentage of deployed regulating megawatts to 

cleared regulating megawatts for each product, the resource must buy back the non-deployed 

megawatts at the mileage marginal clearing price for the respective product. If the mileage 

marginal clearing price used for the buyback is greater than the unit’s cost for the product a 

make-whole payment may be granted. 

Figure 4–32, below, illustrates the mileage make-whole payments for 2024 and the prior two 

years.  

State of the Market 2024 

142 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Market prices and costs 

Figure 4–32  Regulation mileage make-whole payments 

Regulation-up mileage make-whole payments were just above $500,000 in 2024, up 26% from 

2023. Regulation-down mileage make-whole payments were over $600,000 in 2024, down 60% 

from 2023. The design deficiency described in Section 4.1.6 can be directly attributed to the 

disparity between the regulation-down and regulation-up mileage make-whole payments seen 

in Figure 4–32. This design inefficiency is one of the main contributors to the disparity between 

the mileage make-whole payments paid out to regulation-up and regulation-down.  

  DISTRIBUTION OF MARGINAL LOSSES (OVER -COLLECTED 

LOSS REVENUE) 

Both the congestion and loss components of prices create excess revenues for SPP that must be 

distributed to market participants in an economically efficient manner. In the case of marginal 

loss revenues, this requires that the distribution does not alter market incentives.  

The current design consolidates the distributions of day-ahead and real-time over-collected loss 
rebates into one distribution.82 Both day-ahead and real-time over-collected loss rebates are 

distributed on only real-time withdrawing megawatts. This includes loads, substation power, 
exports, wheel-throughs, pseudo-ties, and bilateral settlement schedules (BSS).83  

Over-collected losses for the past three years are shown in Figure 4–33.

Section 4.2.3.

underlying megawatts associated with the BSS are not less than the megawatts of the BSS.

A total of $140 million was paid out in over-collected losses rebates during 2024, with $133

  REVENUE NEUTRALITY UPLIFT 

Revenue neutrality uplift (RNU), shown in Figure 4–34, ensures settlement payments and 

receipts for each hourly settlement interval equal zero. Positive revenue neutrality uplift 

indicates that SPP receives insufficient revenue and collects from market participants. Inversely, a 

negative revenue-neutrality uplift would indicate that SPP received excess revenue that must be 

credited back to market participants. 

On October 16, 2024, Revision Request 59684 was implemented, which removed the real-time 

inadvertent energy amount and real-time congestion amount and included them with the real-

time revenue inadequacy amount. Prior to this revision request, the inadvertent energy amount 

and the congestion amount were removed from the overall real-time revenue inadequacy 

amount. The reasoning behind this revision request is that it will remove redundant settlement 

calculations from revenue neutrality uplift that are not needed in the financial settlement 

process.  

84 https://www.spp.org/Documents/70440/RR596.zip  

State of the Market 2024 

144 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Figure 4–34  Revenue neutrality uplift 

Market prices and costs 

Total monthly revenue neutrality uplift for 2024 was $163 million, down 21% from $205 million 

in 2023. The main components of revenue neutrality uplift are real-time congestion, revenue 

inadequacy, and real-time joint operating agreement (also known as market-to-market). The 

joint operating agreement component generally acts to lessen revenue neutrality uplift, while 

the congestion and revenue inadequacy components generally act to increase revenue 

neutrality uplift. Real-time joint operating agreement portion of revenue neutrality uplift was 

just over $112 million in 2024, an increase of 20% from $94 million in 2023. The real-time 

congestion and revenue inadequacy components of revenue neutrality uplift was $257 million in 

2024, a decrease of 9% from $283 million in 2023.

Production cost “is defined as the settlement cost for the market … for all resources.”87 

Production cost, in this case, is the sum of four components: 

•  energy: cleared megawatts multiplied by locational marginal prices; 

•  ancillary service: cleared operating reserves multiplied by market clearing prices; 

• 

start-up: “… the out of pocket cost that a Market Participant incurs in starting up a 
generating unit from an off-line state …;”88 and 

•  no-load: “… the hourly fee for operating a synchronized Resource at zero … output.”89

4.4  LONG-RUN PRICE SIGNALS FOR INVESTMENT 

In the long term, market prices provide signals for investment in new transmission and 

generation, as well as ongoing maintenance of existing generation and transmission assets to 

meet load. Given the relatively low average SPP market prices since the beginning of the market, 

the MMU does not expect SPP market prices by themselves to support new entry of generation 

investments. While the SPP market has historically offered low incentives for new generation, 

some reasons for new generation investments include expansion of corporate renewable goals, 

bilateral contracts, purchase power agreements, SPP market protocol requirements, federal 

and/or state incentives, state-regulated investments, emerging technologies, and emission 

reduction plans. However, stakeholder reactions and feedback to SPP’s Board of Directors 2022 

approval of a three-percentage point increase in the Planning Reserve Margin (PRM) suggests 

that these out-of-market factors are not significant enough to drive enough investment in new 

generation to keep up with SPP’s reliability needs. 

To determine the extent market prices could support new investments, the MMU analyzed the 

fixed costs, including annual fixed operating and maintenance costs, of five generation 
technologies relative to their potential net revenues,90 at SPP market prices. The generator types 

include scrubbed coal, natural gas combined-cycle, industrial frame natural gas combustion 

turbine, wind, and solar photovoltaic with storage. This analysis uses daily averages and 

assumptions to estimate profitability of constructing new generation and supporting the on-

going cost of a new generator. It does not reflect the profitability of any specific existing 

generator. Many costs, such as supply adequacy penalties and individual contracts, are not 

included in this analysis. 

This is a very high-level analysis that uses several assumptions and averages. Daily averages at a 

hub were used for gas prices. A single gas hub was assumed for each region. These gas prices 

did not account for bilateral trades, hedges or any additional charges not represented by the 

hub price. Energy prices used were daily unweighted averages across the footprint. Figure 4–38

Overwhelmingly, the conclusions do not vary geographically, despite differing energy prices and 

fuel costs. Historically, the SPS region has had higher net revenues than other regions for gas 

resources. This is likely because combined-cycle plants and combustion turbines in the Permian 

Basin (West Texas) region consistently experience below average natural gas prices. In 2024, 

these gas prices were significantly lower than usual, in most cases, at or below $0/MMBtu. This 

resulted in net revenue covering the cost of new entry for SPS. 

Based on these results, the MMU expects the market to continue to signal the retirement of 

some coal generation while also not signaling the long-term investment of other types of new 

generation. A decrease in overall available capacity—along with observed higher outages—and 

changes in the generation fleet profile could present challenges for reliability. 

External economic decisions can provide additional impetus needed for new generation 

investments, such as the expansion of corporate renewable goals, SPP out-of-market payments, 

bilateral contracts, purchase power agreements, SPP market protocol requirements, federal 

State of the Market 2024 

151 

 
 
 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Market prices and costs 

and/or state incentives, state-regulated investments, emerging technologies, and/or emission 

reduction plans. However, market prices, by themselves, have mostly not signaled new 

generation entry since the inception of the Integrated Marketplace. Other revenue streams for 

value added could change this conclusion. For instance, resources could be paid to ensure their 

generation is highly dependable, or resources could be paid to remain available for specific 

performance requirements in the short to medium timeframe. As the market is currently 

designed, it does not incentivize new entry for energy capacity.  

Out-of-market actions by SPP operators, and the resultant make-whole payments, for reliability 

(manual) commitments reflect some of the symptomatic issues. If these make-whole payments 

were represented in a market price for energy or ancillary services, then the out-of-market 

payments would likely decrease, and market-based prices would likely increase. The increase in 

market prices would support net revenue for all capacity resources with marginal costs below 

that of the marginal unit[s] setting price in any interval. This suggests that, while important, 

market policies which maximize the ability of operators to minimize uplift in lieu of better price 

formation in the markets would, by themselves, not solve the money gap needed to incent new 

generation builds.

Southwest Power Pool, Inc.

Congestion and transmission congestion rights 

5  CONGESTION AND TRANSMISSION CONGESTION 

RIGHTS MARKET 

This chapter reviews transmission congestion in the SPP market footprint, as well as the 

transmission congestion rights market in the Integrated Marketplace. Key points from this 

chapter include: 

•  The areas that experienced the highest congestion costs in 2024 were around Oklahoma 

City and south-central Oklahoma in the southeast part of the footprint, southeast New 

Mexico in the southwest part of the footprint, western Nebraska towards the middle of 

the footprint, and northwest North Dakota and southeast North Dakota towards the 

north of the footprint. Much of Kansas, Nebraska, and northwest Oklahoma experienced 

the lowest congestion costs for the year. 

•  The footprint again saw frequent congestion on flowgates located in Oklahoma, with 

four of the top ten flowgates in 2024 located in Oklahoma. The flowgate with the highest 

congestion costs in 2024 was the Cimarron transformer to the west of Oklahoma City. 

While congestion present in 2023 within northwest North Dakota again occurred in 2024, 

much of this congestion abated after March 2024. Additionally, new pockets in southeast 

New Mexico and western Nebraska emerged in 2024. 

• 

Intervals having no congestion were again rare in the 2024 day-ahead but more than 

doubled in real-time year-over-year. Overall, 2024 had fewer real-time intervals than 

2023 that had no congestion, and slightly more intervals that were binding or contained 

a breached constraint. 

•  The 2024 frequently constrained area study identified the Tulsa, Oklahoma; Oklahoma 

City, Oklahoma; and Williston, North Dakota areas as frequently constrained areas and 

these areas continued to see elevated congestion prices during 2024. The Joplin, 

Missouri study area did not qualify as a frequently constrained area in the most recent 

study. Additionally, while the Williston, North Dakota area surfaced during the most 

recent frequently constrained area study period (March 1, 2023 through February 29, 

2024), its primary constraint experienced significantly lower shadow prices as the year 

2024 progressed.  

• 

In aggregate, load-serving entities were over-hedged with TCR and ARR funding at 158% 

of their congestion cost, non-load-serving entities only hedged 55% of their total 

congestion cost. 

State of the Market 2024 

153 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Congestion and transmission congestion rights 

• 

Individual market participants hedged congestion with varying degrees of effectiveness. 

Overall, 79% of load-serving entities recovered all their congestion cost through the ARR 

and TCR auctions.  

•  Total congestion payments for 2024 were over $1.8 billion, up from $1.4 billion in 2023 

but still down from $2 billion in 2022. 

•  Transmission congestion rights (TCR) funding in 2024 fell outside the target range of 

90% to 100% again, coming in at 83%, down from the 88% seen in 2022 and 2023. The 

total TCR shortfall for 2024 was $449,000, an increase of 85% from the $243,000 net 

shortfall seen in 2023. 

•  Auction revenue right (ARR) funding increased to 125%, up from 118% in 2023. 

Relatedly, the ARR surplus increased by just under $45 million year over year. 

•  Participants can transfer congestion rights through use of a bulletin board or selling back 

positions in the auction. However, most congestion rights are not transferred or sold. 
Intra-auction sales94 increased from 6.3% of the total auction volume in 2023 to 7.7% in 

2024. There were no bulletin board trades in 2024. 

5.1  TRANSMISSION CONGESTION  

The locational marginal price (LMP) for approximately 1,300 settlement locations in the SPP 

market reflects the sum of three components: 

1)   marginal energy component (MEC) - system-wide marginal cost of the energy 

required to serve the market, 

2)   marginal congestion component (MCC) - the marginal cost of any increase or 

decrease in energy at a location with respect to transmission constraints, and 

3)   marginal loss component (MLC) - the marginal cost of any increase or decrease in 

energy to minimize system transmission losses. 

𝐿𝑀𝑃 = 𝑀𝐸𝐶 + 𝑀𝐶𝐶 + 𝑀𝐿𝐶 

LMPs are a key feature of electricity markets that ensure the efficient scheduling, commitment, 

and dispatch of generation given the system load and reliability constraints. LMPs also provide 

price signals to efficiently incentivize future generation and transmission investment and to help 

guide retirement decisions. 

94 The sale of a previously acquired position in a subsequent auction.  

State of the Market 2024 

154 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Congestion and transmission congestion rights 

This section focuses on the congestion and loss components of price and related items 

including: 

•  geographic pattern of congestion and losses, 

•  changes in the transmission system that alter congestion patterns, 

•  congestion impacts on local market power, 

• 

load-serving entities hedging congestion costs in the transmission congestion rights 

market, and 

•  distribution of marginal congestion and loss amounts. 

  PRICING PATTERNS AND CONGESTION  

Figure 5–1 shows price contour maps representing the day-ahead and real-time average prices 

in 2023. 

Figure 5–1  Price map, day-ahead and real-time market 

State of the Market 2024 

155 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Congestion and transmission congestion rights 

Annual average day-ahead market prices ranged from around $11/MWh in western Kansas and 

western Oklahoma to up $55/MWh in the northwest section of North Dakota, southeast New 

Mexico, and south-central Oklahoma. Lower average day-ahead prices tend to coincide with 

pricing nodes associated with wind generators in locations such as those to the west of 

Oklahoma City and in central Kansas. Just over 41% of the 1,309 settlement locations had an 

annual average day-ahead market price between $20/MWh and $30/MWh. 

Congestion accounted for about 92% of the price variation in 2024, surpassing the 89% figure 

from 2023. This is the highest percentage in price variation due to congestion since the 

beginning of the Integrated Marketplace in 2014. Marginal losses accounted for 8% of the price 

variation in 2024 compared to 11% in 2023.  

In the day-ahead market, settlement location average prices ranged from −$4/MWh to 

$65/MWh, and in the real-time market, settlement location average prices ranged from 

−$16/MWh to $71/MWh. Transmission buildout has allowed higher levels of low-cost wind 

generation in the western parts of the SPP footprint to serve load centers located in the eastern 

portions of SPP. Congestion remains on the southeastern edge of SPP extending from northern 

Missouri to southern Oklahoma which signifies the boundary between locations of inexpensive 

renewable and conventional generation.  

  CONGESTION BY GEOGRAPHIC LOCATION  

The major drivers of congestion patterns in SPP are the physical characteristics of the 

transmission grid and associated transfer capability, the geographic distribution of load, the 

geographic differences in fuel costs, and external flows from neighboring areas. The eastern side 

of the SPP footprint, with a higher concentration of load, also has a higher concentration of 

high-voltage (345 kV) transmission lines. Historically, high-voltage connections between the 

west and east have been limited; transmission buildout has resulted in most congestion 

occurring on the southeastern edge of the SPP footprint.  

The cost of coal-fired generation increases as transportation costs rise. For example, 

transportation cost increases with distance from the Wyoming Powder River Basin near the 

northwest corner of SPP’s footprint. This is important because even though it is declining over 

time, coal still accounted for 22% of SPP’s installed capacity and 23% of energy generation in 

2024. 

Natural gas-fired generation, SPP’s largest fuel type by installed capacity (36% in 2024), resides 

predominantly in the southern portions of SPP. Wind-powered generation generally lies in the 

western half of the footprint, and nuclear generation resides near the center, while the majority 

of hydro is in the north. 

State of the Market 2024 

156 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Congestion and transmission congestion rights 

These factors combine to create a general northwest to southeast split in prices. This general 

northwest to southeast pricing split does exist in 2024 pricing patterns with congestion in the 

Central and southeast Oklahoma regions. However, some constraints located in northwest North 

Dakota, southeast North Dakota, and western Nebraska create congestion pockets that act as 

exceptions to this trend. Additionally, constraints in southeast New Mexico created a large 

congestion pocket in 2024 that was not present in 2023. 

Figure 5–2 depicts the average marginal congestion component for the day-ahead market 

across the SPP footprint in 2024. 

Figure 5–2  Marginal congestion cost map, day-ahead market 

The lowest average day-ahead marginal congestion costs occurred around central Kansas, 

central Oklahoma, and the southern part of Nebraska panhandle. These regions averaged near 

negative $30/MWh. The highest average day-ahead marginal congestion costs exist near 

northwest North Dakota, south-central Oklahoma, and southeast New Mexico. Average day-

ahead marginal congestion costs reached nearly $25/MWh in 2024.

  TRANSMISSION EXPANSION  

During the year 2024, there were 43 completed transmission upgrades totaling almost $162 
million (shown on Figure 5–3 below).95 These completed upgrades will support the efficient 

transmission of energy across the SPP footprint and promote reliability. A list of these projects is 
included in the 2025 SPP Transmission Expansion Plan report.96 SPP’s Transmission Planning 

processes such as the Integrated Transmission Planning assessment, Generation Interconnection 

studies, Delivery Point assessments, and Transmission Service studies identify needs for 

transmission projects. The Integrated Transmission Planning assessment identified the projects 

in Figure 5–3 and the projects depicted on the map in Figure 5–4 are projects that will further 

enhance the SPP transmission grid in future years.

Congestion and transmission congestion rights 

The planned projects in Figure 5–4 and Figure 5–13 will support the efficient transmission of 

energy across the SPP footprint and promote reliability, as well as potentially provide relief for 

the most congested areas in SPP. However, recent analysis by SPP staff has shown significant 

delays of several transmission projects, which will slow the benefits of these upgrades and can 

result in increased congestion costs. The transparency of these delays has been limited. 

Therefore, the MMU made recommendations for improving situational awareness of 

transmission upgrades and improving the process to reassign projects should transmission 
owners be unable to perform the upgrades in a reasonable timeframe.97 SPP has taken measures 

to both increase the transparency and escalate reassignment needs through the stakeholder 

process.  

Two transmission projects, Roundup to Kummer Ridge and Finstad to Tande to Leland Olds, 

have been constructed or are nearing completion and should help reduce congestion in 

Northwest North Dakota. Roundup to Kummer Ridge was energized in December of 2024, and 

the Leland Olds Station-to-Tande is planned to come online in late 2026. Additionally, a 600 

MW addition of resource capacity is also planned in the area by Basin Electric Power 

Cooperative whose first phase of the build is not expected to be completed until 2025. Lastly, a 

remedial action scheme was implemented in March 2024 to mitigate congestion on the Charlie 

Creek to Watford City constraint, which has significantly decreased congestion prices in the area. 

  TRANSMISSION CONSTRAINTS 

Market congestion reflects the economic dispatch cost of honoring transmission constraints. 

SPP uses these constraints to manage the flow of energy across the physical bottlenecks of the 

grid in the least costly manner while ensuring reliability. In doing so, SPP calculates a shadow 

price for each constraint, which shows the potential reduction in the total market production 

costs if the constraint limit were increased by one megawatt for one hour. Figure 5–5 provides 

the top 10 constraints by shadow price for 2024.

  GEOGRAPHY AND MARGINAL LOSSES 

Variable transmission line losses decrease with increased line voltage or decreased line length 

for the same amount of power moved. In the SPP footprint, much of the low-cost generation 

resides at a distance from the load and with limited high-voltage interconnection. The average 

variable losses on the SPP system for 2024 were 2.8% in the day-ahead market. This is down 

slightly from 2.9%in 2023 and 3.2% in 2022. The marginal loss component of the price captures 

the change in the total system cost of losses with an additional increment of load at a particular 

location relative to the reference bus. 

Figure 5–14 maps the annual average day-ahead market marginal loss components for 2024. 

State of the Market 2024 

166 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Congestion and transmission congestion rights 

Figure 5–14  Marginal loss component map, day-ahead 

The average day-ahead marginal loss component ranges from about −$3.72 MWh in the Texas 

Panhandle to almost $3.65/MWh in eastern Nebraska. Negative values reduce prices through 

the marginal loss component relative to the marginal energy cost. Positive values increase prices 

as generation from these locations are more beneficial from a marginal loss perspective. The 

$7.38/MWh spread between geographic prices in 2024 is similar to the $7/MWh spread from 

2023. 

  FREQUENTLY CONSTRAINED AREAS AND LOCAL MARKET 

POWER 

Congestion in the market creates geographic areas where only a limited number of suppliers 

can provide the energy to serve load in an area without overloading a constrained transmission 

element. Under these circumstances, the pivotal suppliers have local market power and the 

ability to raise prices above competitive levels or reduce output below optimal levels. SPP’s tariff 

provides provisions for mitigating the impact of local market power on prices, and the 

effectiveness of market power mitigation is described in Section 6.2.2. Local market power can 

State of the Market 2024 

167 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Congestion and transmission congestion rights 

be either transitory, as is frequently the case with an outage, or persistent, when a particular area 

is frequently import-constrained. 

Since the SPP tariff calls for more stringent market power mitigation for frequently constrained 

areas, the MMU analyzes market data at least annually to assess the appropriateness of the 
frequently constrained area designations. The 2023 study98 period was March 1, 2023, through 
February 29, 2024, and identified a total of three areas as frequently constrained.99 Both the 

number of pivotal supplier hours and magnitude of congestion decreased in the 2023 study 

period when compared to the 2022 study period. The Tulsa, Oklahoma frequently constrained 

area consists of three constraints and thirty-seven resources while the Oklahoma City, Oklahoma 

frequently constrained area consists of twelve constraints and forty-four resources. The 

Williston, North Dakota frequently constrained area consists of one constraint and twenty-six 

resources. Joplin, Missouri was designated as a frequently constrained area in 2022 and was 

removed in the 2023 study for not meeting criteria on the number of real-time pivotal supplier 

hours. While Joplin was removed from the list, it did come close to meeting frequently 

constrained area designation criteria by having over 500 binding hours meeting the $25/MWh 

impact threshold. The 2024 study is currently being performed.  

  MARKET CONGESTION MANAGEMENT 

In optimizing the flow of energy to serve the load at the least cost, the SPP market makes 

extensive use of the available transmission up to constraint limits. When constraints reach their 

limits, they are considered binding. The market occasionally allows transmission lines to exceed 
their rating if the price to correct the overload becomes too high.100 This is considered a 

breached constraint. Figure 5–15 highlights day-ahead market binding, breached, and 

uncongested intervals.

breached condition. While 2024 did not see the same number of breached intervals as 2022, the

  CONGESTION PAYMENTS AND UPLIFTS  

Market participants in the energy market incur congestion costs and receive congestion 

payments based on their marginal impact on total market congestion cost through the marginal 

congestion component of price. Most SPP market participants owning physical assets are 

vertically integrated, so their net congestion cost depends on two things. The first is whether 

they are a net buyer or seller of energy. The second is the relative marginal cost component at 

their generation and load. For financial market participants, congestion costs reflect the impact 

of virtual positions on a binding or breached constraint in the day-ahead and real-time markets.
